# Research Note: Tenant isolation for Kodeebo (hosted backend, local-first cashier devices, owner dashboard and Android app)

> **Path**: `docs/research/tenant-isolation.md` — written **before** the first line of production code (`research-protocol.md`).
> **Status**: Draft
> **Author**: six `researcher` parts run in two waves (parts 1-3, then 4-6), merged by `/research` on 2026-10-17
> **Date**: 2026-10-17
> **Feature / story**: none yet (no `design/specs/`); `design/product-brief.md` (Tier 1 surfaces, items 5, 7, 9, 10, 11; decision log 2026-10-16: hosted multi-tenant only)
> **Triggers**: new feature · auth (device credential, sessions, roles) · crypto (device store, signed requests) · PII (staff data under PDPL) — feeds `/threat-model tenant-isolation` and the proposed `local-first-sync` threat model
> **Parts and ID blocks**: part *n* owns findings `F{n}01–F{n}99` and spikes `S{n}0–S{n}9`; foreign IDs carry a prefix: `restaurant-menu A13` (`~/saas-products/restaurant-menu/docs/research/tenant-isolation.md`, read-only input, never edited), `sync-note F124` (`docs/research/local-first-sync.md`), `desktop-note F112` (`docs/research/desktop-client-technology.md`), `eta-note F405` (`docs/research/eta-e-receipt-e-invoice.md`), `part-3 F329` (a part of this note). The merge concatenates the parts without renumbering: 204 finding rows (29 + 50 + 32 + 33 + 36 + 24), no ID used twice.
> **Evidence (tier E)**: `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-1/` … `part-6/` — scripts, outputs and READMEs per part; wrong or confounded runs are kept and labelled by the parts.
> **Run record**: fetch budgets 15 / 20 / 20 / 20 / 30 / 30. Parts 2 and 3 hit the 40-turn limit and were resumed once with no further fetching; part 2 had written no findings before the resume and wrote them from its saved evidence. Parts 4-6 were interrupted by an API rate limit and resumed from their transcripts. Part 6 did not read parts 4 and 5 (merge check in § 4.2 item 11).

## 1. Question

What mechanisms keep each Kodeebo tenant's data, devices and people isolated — on the hosted PostgreSQL 18.6 backend, on the sync endpoint and its server inbox `(tenant_id, device_id, command_id)`, inside a tenant across branches, on the cashier device's encrypted store, through tenant export and deletion, and under load from other tenants — and how is each one proved by a cross-tenant negative test? The answer feeds: the `cto` ADR on the tenancy model (`tenancy.model` in `project.yaml`), the tenant-context mechanism for whichever backend track `/setup-stack` picks (Go/pgx or a JVM driver; this note stays neutral), the device credential and key-custody ADRs, the retention/deletion ADR and counsel brief, and `/threat-model`. Sync protocol facts are **not** re-researched; they are linked from `docs/research/local-first-sync.md` (sync protocol, SC-19 restore watermark § 9.1.2, OQ6, OQ29). Each part's own question follows unchanged.

### Part 1 — PostgreSQL RLS re-verification at 18.6; tenancy model options

Which tenancy model should Kodeebo's hosted multi-tenant backend use — `shared-schema-rls`, `schema-per-tenant` or `db-per-tenant` (`project.yaml tenancy.model`, currently unset) — given three venues today, unknown growth, an anchor price of 10,000 EGP/year per venue, local-first Windows cashier devices that sync commands into a server inbox, an owner web dashboard and an owner Android app? The decision is the `cto`'s (ADR); this note feeds that ADR, the `/threat-model tenant-isolation` and later `/setup-stack` (Go or Ktor undecided). Part 1 covers two areas only: (1) re-verifying at PostgreSQL 18.6 the PostgreSQL facts that the sibling product's note (restaurant-menu) relies on — RLS with ENABLE+FORCE, runtime roles without BYPASSRLS/superuser/ownership, pooler-safe per-transaction tenant context, unique/FK checks bypassing RLS, `security_invoker` views, and the catalogue query proving every tenant table has ENABLE+FORCE; (2) the three model options with cost, risk, performance, operability and their interaction with per-tenant export, deletion and restoring one tenant alone. Drivers, proxies, device binding, branches, export/deletion mechanics and tests belong to other parts.

### Part 2 — Tenant context per backend track (pgx; pgjdbc, HikariCP, Exposed, jOOQ); edge proxy (Caddy, Traefik)

Decision informed: the tenant-isolation design for Kodeebo (hosted multi-tenant backend, PostgreSQL 18.6; RLS with a transaction-local tenant setting is the leading candidate; the model itself is decided later by a `cto` ADR; the backend track, Go or Ktor, is decided at `/setup-stack`). This part answers two things only. (1) Which driver-level mechanisms set a per-transaction tenant context that is safe behind a transaction-mode pooler and cannot leak from one request to the next, on the Go track (pgx 5.11.0 / pgxpool) and the JVM track (pgjdbc, HikariCP 7.1.0, Exposed 1.5.1, jOOQ as alternative, coroutine pitfalls); and, for each track, how a use case that forgets the context fails closed and which test proves it. (2) Which edge-proxy settings strip client-sent tenant and forwarding headers, give the true client IP and redact access logs for Caddy 2.11.7 (re-verifying restaurant-menu Part C) and Traefik 3.7.14 (new), since the proxy is not chosen. Deciders: `cto` (ADR), `platform-lead` / `infra-engineer` (proxy). Out of scope (other parts): RLS semantics, tenancy model, device binding, branches, export/deletion, test plan.

### Part 3 — Device enrolment and credential; sync request scoping; server-side sync state

Kodeebo's Windows cashier device syncs with a hosted multi-tenant backend (PostgreSQL 18.6). This note informs the `cto` tenancy ADR, the sync ADRs (write path; N5 lease and key custody) and `/threat-model` on two things. **(1) Device binding:** how a cashier device is enrolled to exactly one tenant and one branch (enrolment code or owner-approved pairing, one-time, expiring); which credential it then presents (device key pair generated on the device versus a server-issued secret; mTLS client certificate versus signed requests such as RFC 9421 versus DPoP RFC 9449 versus a bearer device token); how the private key is held (DPAPI / TPM per desktop-note F112-F114); so that `tenant_id`, `branch_id` and `device_id` of every sync request come from the authenticated credential and never from the payload; what the server does with a payload whose ids disagree (founder rule OQ6: refuse, record a security event, alert the owner); how revocation, re-registration and rotation work; and how the inbox key `(tenant_id, device_id, command_id)` and the unique `(tenant_id, device_id, device_seq)` are filled from the credential. **(2) Server-side sync state:** the verified-restore watermark (SC-19, OQ29), offline-period records, device events (seq_conflict, clone/restore suspicion, server-behind-device) and the owner review queue for flagged money facts (OQ6), each as a tenant-scoped (and branch-scoped where it applies) table under RLS, with the actor that writes and reads each and whether it must survive a server restore. Decider: `cto` (ADR) with the founder; recommendations only here. Tier 1 is one device per branch; the design must not block several devices per branch later.

### Part 4 — Branch scoping and session; encrypted local store binding and re-registration

Kodeebo is sold to venue owners, some of whom run a chain (one owner, two or more branches). Two decisions need sourced facts. **(1) Per-branch scoping inside a tenant:** what data model joins tenant → branch → device with staff roles owner / manager / cashier and a branch assignment (brief item 5, W5); where branch scope is enforced (a second transaction-local setting under PostgreSQL RLS, application-layer authorisation, or both); how a manager assigned to branch A is denied branch B in the owner dashboard (static Vite+React SPA) and in the owner Android app API; how the owner gets cross-branch aggregated reports; how the dashboard/Android session token carries tenant and branch scope without ever trusting a client-sent branch id; and how that composes with part-1's tenancy recommendation (shared-schema RLS, pending decision) and part-3's device binding (a device is bound to one branch). **(2) The encrypted local store on a cashier device:** one tenant and one branch per device store; what is bound to the tenant (store file, data-key wrap, device credential); what happens when the same PC is re-registered to another tenant or branch (wipe versus crypto-erase by destroying the wrapped key; whether unsynced commands of the old tenant must drain first or be exported; refusing to open a store whose tenant id disagrees with the credential; consequences for the ETA per-device receipt chain); and what residual data stays on disk after a crypto-erase (SQLite free pages, WAL/journal, OS backups and shadow copies) given key custody per desktop-note F112-F116. Deciders: `cto` (tenancy ADR, key-custody ADR) with the founder; `product-manager` for the role model. This note recommends and decides nothing.

### Part 5 — Tenant export and deletion (PDPL, ETA retention); noisy-neighbour limits on sync

Two areas, both feeding the `cto` tenancy ADR and the retention/deletion ADR. (1) **Export and deletion of a tenant's data** (hosted, PostgreSQL 18.6, Egypt): what Personal Data Protection Law No. 151 of 2020 (and its executive regulation, if issued and in force) says about data-subject rights, controller versus processor roles (venue owner as controller for staff attendance and passcodes, Kodelix as hosting processor), breach notification and retention limits; how a tenant deletion request reconciles with the five-year tax-record duty of Unified Tax Procedures Law 206/2020 (eta-note F405, F406); what a tenant export must contain and in which format; how deletion reaches backups and PITR (expiry with the backup window versus per-tenant crypto-shredding); and what happens to the devices of a deleted or suspended tenant. (2) **Noisy-neighbour limits on the sync endpoint**: per-tenant and per-device rate limits and quotas (batch size, request rate, concurrent syncs, bytes, catch-up after days offline without throttling a legitimate burst into data loss), where they are enforced (edge proxy, application, Valkey/Redis-protocol counter), the algorithm (token bucket, GCRA, fixed window), the response contract (RFC 9457 problem, `Retry-After`, `RateLimit` headers and their IETF status), per-tenant PostgreSQL limits, and how limits are stored as data per plan. Not covered here: the tenancy model choice, drivers, device enrolment, branch roles, the device's local store, the test plan (other parts). Recommendations only; the `cto` decides (ADR), the user decides scope, counsel decides every legal reading.

### Part 6 — Cross-tenant negative test catalogue; ASVS / MASVS / API Top 10 mapping

Which negative tests must Kodeebo carry so that "tenant isolation tested" (DoD gate 7, `security-baseline.md`) is true for three surfaces: (a) the sync endpoint used by the Windows cashier device, (b) the owner dashboard API behind the static SPA, and (c) the owner Android app, plus the database layer under all three? For each test: preconditions, action, expected result, layer (unit / integration with Testcontainers / contract / Playwright / Android instrumented) and the tool at our pins, in a backend-track-neutral form with the Go and Ktor tool named separately. Second, which official requirement IDs do these tests evidence (OWASP ASVS 5.0.0, MASVS 2.1.0 with MASTG tests where they exist, API Security Top 10 2023), and which IDs that wave-1 parts left NOT SOURCEABLE can now be resolved from the official texts. The note informs `/threat-model` (`security-lead`), `/qa-plan` (`qa-lead`), `/tenant-check` and the stories' acceptance criteria; the `cto` tenancy ADR is the decision these tests verify, not one this note makes. The tenancy model is unset (part-1 recommends shared schema with row-level security, pending decision): every test is written as a black-box outcome except the DB group (DB-xx), which assumes shared-schema RLS and says where it would change.

## 3. Findings

Concatenated unchanged from the six parts (each part's own sub-headings are demoted one level; § references inside a part refer to that part). Finding rows per part, checked against the part files: part 1 = 29 (F101-F129), part 2 = 50, part 3 = 32 (F301-F332), part 4 = 33, part 5 = 36 (F501-F536), part 6 = 24 (F601-F624); 204 in total.

### Part 1 — PostgreSQL RLS re-verification at 18.6; tenancy model options

Tier: 1 normative · 2 official docs · 3 official source/changelog/advisory · 4 engineering writing · E executed here (scripts and outputs under `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-1/`, run 2026-10-16 UTC, PostgreSQL 18.6, container `ti-p1-pg` started by this session, removed at the end) · "internal" = a project file, not a source tier. Each reused fact names the restaurant-menu ID it repeats.

#### 3.1 Re-verification of the PostgreSQL facts the restaurant-menu note relies on

| # | Claim | Source (link or evidence) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------------------|------|-------|
| F101 | Superusers and `BYPASSRLS` roles always bypass row security; a table owner bypasses it unless `ALTER TABLE ... FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY`; only the owner can enable row security or add policies. | [PG 18 ddl-rowsecurity](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/ddl-rowsecurity.html) | 2 | Repeats restaurant-menu A1 (same source). The page does not mention views, `set_config`, pooling or `pg_dump`; those are covered by F107, F109, F112, F116. |
| F102 | With row security on and no policy the default is deny; `TRUNCATE` and `REFERENCES` are not subject to row security; unique, primary-key and foreign-key checks always bypass row security, so schema design must avoid covert channels. Policy expressions run before user conditions except for `leakproof` functions. | [PG 18 ddl-rowsecurity](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/ddl-rowsecurity.html) | 2 | Repeats restaurant-menu A19; sync-note F110 cites the same page. Proved by F105 and F106. |
| F103 | Executed, role `migrator` (NOLOGIN, owns all tables), `app_rt` (LOGIN, NOSUPERUSER, NOBYPASSRLS, not owner), `bypass_t` (LOGIN, BYPASSRLS), 3 rows in `orders` (2 for tenant A, 1 for B), context set to B: owner with ENABLE only saw 3 (bypass); owner with ENABLE+FORCE saw 1, and 0 with no context; the BYPASSRLS role saw 3; `app_rt` saw 0 with no context, 2 as A, 1 as B; `pg_roles` showed `app_rt` rolsuper f / rolbypassrls f / rolcreaterole f / rolcreatedb f and `pg_class` showed `migrator` as owner of every table. | `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-1/s10-rls-reverify.sql`, output `s10-output.txt` (section T1), command `bash run-s10.sh`, `postgres:18.6`, 2026-10-16 | E | Caveat: roles were assumed with `SET [LOCAL] ROLE` from a superuser session (RLS is decided on the current user); S12 re-checks `app_rt` as a real login through a pooler (F115). ENABLE+FORCE and no bypass attribute reproduce restaurant-menu A1/A14-style claims at 18.6. |
| F104 | Executed: as `app_rt`, `ALTER TABLE orders DISABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` and `CREATE POLICY` both failed with "must be owner of table orders"; an INSERT carrying another tenant's `tenant_id` failed with "new row violates row-level security policy". | `s10-output.txt` (T1, last three checks), same script | E | Confirms F101 for a non-owner runtime role. |
| F105 | Executed: a unique key that does not lead with `tenant_id` lets tenant B learn that tenant A holds a value (B's insert of `shared-code` failed with `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "gunique_code_key"` although B sees none of A's rows); `UNIQUE (tenant_id, code)` accepted the same value for B. | `s10-output.txt` (T3a, T3b) | E | Agrees with sync-note F128 (tenant-leading inbox key) and F102. Rule: every unique index on a tenant table leads with `tenant_id`. |
| F106 | Executed: with a single-column foreign key, tenant B inserted a child row pointing at tenant A's parent id 100 (B sees 0 parent rows) and the insert **succeeded** (a cross-tenant reference), while a pointer to a non-existent id 999 failed with a FK violation, so the difference is an existence oracle. With a composite FK `(tenant_id, parent_id) REFERENCES parent(tenant_id, id)` the pointer to A's parent failed with the same FK error as a missing parent, and B's own parent worked. | `s10-output.txt` (T3c-T3g) | E | Goes beyond restaurant-menu A19 (which states only the bypass). Rule for the schema: every FK between tenant tables is composite with `tenant_id`, which needs `UNIQUE (tenant_id, id)` on the parent. |
| F107 | Views check the base tables with the **view owner's** privileges and policies by default; with `security_invoker = true` the invoking user's privileges and policies apply, as for a direct query; the option does not change how functions inside the view run; it can be set with `CREATE VIEW ... WITH (...)` or changed with `ALTER VIEW`. | [PG 18 CREATE VIEW](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-createview.html) | 2 | Repeats restaurant-menu A18. |
| F108 | Executed: with context B and `app_rt` selecting, a view owned by the BYPASSRLS role returned **3** rows (cross-tenant leak); the same view with `security_invoker = true` returned 1; a view owned by the table owner under FORCE returned 1. `pg_class.reloptions` held `{security_invoker=true}`; a catalogue test that lists views whose `reloptions` lack `security_invoker=true|on` (case-insensitive) returned exactly `v_plain` and `v_owner_forced`. | `s10-output.txt` (T4, C8), same script | E | The leak needs a view owner that bypasses RLS; with a forced non-bypass owner the same GUC still filters. Create every view over tenant tables `WITH (security_invoker = true)` regardless. restaurant-menu S5 reported the same `reloptions` text issue. |
| F109 | `set_config(name, value, is_local)`: `is_local = true` applies only for the current transaction, `false` for the rest of the session; a NULL value resets to default; `current_setting(name, missing_ok => true)` returns NULL for an unknown setting. The page does not say what the setting reads after a local value ended. | [PG 18 functions-admin](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/functions-admin.html) | 2 | Repeats restaurant-menu B24. |
| F110 | Executed: after `set_config(..., true)` ended by COMMIT and by ROLLBACK, `current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)` in the same session returned the empty string (`[]`), so a policy needs `NULLIF(current_setting(...), '')::uuid`; a second `set_config` in the same transaction silently switched the visible tenant (A: 2 rows, then B: 1 row); a malformed value made the query fail with `invalid input syntax for type uuid`. | `s10-output.txt` (T2), same script | E | Reproduces restaurant-menu B25/B26 at 18.6. "One context per transaction" must be enforced in application code. |
| F111 | Executed: the catalogue queries C1 (tenant-keyed tables lacking ENABLE and FORCE), C2 (no permissive policy for a command), C3 (tables without `tenant_id`), C4 (superuser/BYPASSRLS roles and runtime-role attributes), C5 (relations not owned by `migrator`) and C8 (views without `security_invoker`), as drafted in restaurant-menu §4.4, were run against a schema with 7 correct tenant tables, 3 deliberately broken tenant tables (`bad_enable_only`, `bad_none`, `bad_force_nopolicy`) and 1 global table. C1 returned exactly `bad_enable_only` and `bad_none`; C2 also reported `bad_force_nopolicy` (4 commands each); C3 returned only `global_ref`; C4 returned `postgres` and `bypass_t` and no runtime-role finding; C5 returned 0 rows. | `s10-output.txt` (T5), same script | E | Limits: C1/C3 recognise a tenant table only by a column literally named `tenant_id` (a table keyed otherwise, such as a `tenants` table keyed by `id`, is a C3 row that the allowlist must name — restaurant-menu S5); a passing C1 says nothing about whether the policy expression is correct (restaurant-menu A28); the lookups through SECURITY DEFINER functions (restaurant-menu C9/C10) were not re-run here. |

#### 3.2 Pooling (tenant context behind a pooler)

| # | Claim | Source (link or evidence) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------------------|------|-------|
| F112 | PgBouncer transaction pooling "breaks client expectations of the server by design"; the page marks `SET/RESET`, `LISTEN`, `WITH HOLD` cursors, `PREPARE/DEALLOCATE`, and session-level advisory locks as never working in that mode; protocol-level prepared statements work only when `max_prepared_statements` is non-zero. The page does not mention `SET LOCAL` or `set_config`. | [PgBouncer features](https://www.pgbouncer.org/features.html) | 2 | The page is not versioned and PgBouncer is not pinned. Repeats the point in `docs/stack-reference/postgres/best-practices.md` §1 (internal, Last verified 2026-10-11). `SET LOCAL`/`set_config`: NOT SOURCEABLE from vendor docs — see F115 for the executed result. |
| F113 | PgBouncer defaults: `pool_mode` session; `default_pool_size` 20 per **user/database pair**; `max_db_connections` per PgBouncer database; `max_client_conn` 100 globally; `server_reset_query` (`DISCARD ALL`) is not used in transaction mode unless `server_reset_query_always` is set; `track_extra_parameters` defaults include `search_path`, but before PostgreSQL 18 the server does not report `search_path` changes; a `[databases]` entry may carry its own `pool_size`/`pool_mode`, and `*` is a wildcard fallback entry. | [PgBouncer config](https://www.pgbouncer.org/config.html) | 2 | Basis for the connection arithmetic in §4 (pool partition per database and user). |
| F114 | PgBouncer 1.26.1 (2026-10-14) fixes CVE-2026-107350 (a second StartupMessage during SCRAM authentication when the database does not exist could let a user log in as other SCRAM users, admins included), CVE-2026-107349 and CVE-2026-107348; 1.26.0 (2026-09-23) fixes CVE-2026-19888, CVE-2026-6668, CVE-2026-6669 and starts tracking `search_path` by default on PostgreSQL 18+ (before, a client's `SET search_path` in transaction pooling could affect later clients); 1.25.1 fixed CVE-2025-12819 (arbitrary SQL at authentication via a crafted `search_path` when `track_extra_parameters` includes it and `auth_query` is used); 1.26.1 also lets `ignore_startup_parameters` take precedence and says other `search_path` tracking bugs are to be addressed later. The GitHub advisories page for the PgBouncer repository lists no published advisory. | [PgBouncer changelog](https://www.pgbouncer.org/changelog.html), [GitHub advisories](https://github.com/pgbouncer/pgbouncer/security/advisories) (both fetched 2026-10-17) | 3 | `docs/stack-reference/postgres/best-practices.md` still names 1.26.0 (2026-09-23) as latest: out of date by one release (stack-reference follow-up, §5). The CVE ids appear only on the changelog, not in GHSA. The test in F115 ran 1.26.0, i.e. before these fixes; results about context handling are not a statement about 1.26.1. |
| F115 | Executed, PgBouncer 1.26.0 in transaction mode, pool size 1, clients logging in as `app_rt`: (a) a transaction-local `set_config(...,true)` did not reach the next client — client 2 ran on the same server backend (pid 879 both times), read the setting as `[]` and saw 0 rows; (b) a **session-level** `set_config(...,false)` and a plain `SET app.tenant_id` **did leak**: client 3, which never set a context, ran on pid 879, read the other tenant's id and saw 2 rows (then 1 after the other leak); (c) two concurrent clients with different tenants (A held its transaction 2 s) were serialised and saw 2 and 1 rows; (d) clean rerun on a fresh server connection (S12b): `SET LOCAL` inside a transaction showed 2 rows and the next statement read `[]`/0 rows; `set_config(...,true)` in autocommit left no context for the next statement; after a ROLLBACK, after a client killed mid-transaction and after a malformed-value error (22P02) the next client read no context and saw 0 rows. | `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-1/run-s12.sh` + `s12-output.txt` (run 2), `run-s12b.sh` + `s12b-output.txt` (clean rerun), config `s12-conf/pgbouncer.ini`; `s12-run1-WRONG-TEST-output.txt` / `.ini` / `-note.txt` kept (run 1 failed on auth, no result); 2026-10-16 | E | Run 2's tests T4/T5 ran after the deliberate session-level SET and read the leaked tenant-B value, so their "sees 1" is not a result; S12b is the clean evidence. S12b did not print backend pids, so it cannot tell "rolled back" from "server connection discarded" for the aborted-client case. Does not cover `search_path` or role switching (PENDING — S13). |

#### 3.3 Backup, restore, export and engine facts that decide the model options

| # | Claim | Source (link or evidence) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------------------|------|-------|
| F116 | `pg_dump` dumps one database; by default it sets `row_security` off so every row is dumped, and a role that cannot bypass row security gets an **error** instead of a partial dump; `--enable-row-security` dumps only the rows the role can see (use INSERT format because `COPY FROM` has no row security); `-n`/`-t` dump a subset but make no attempt to include dependencies, so a subset may not restore into a clean database; one database is dumped consistently without blocking readers or writers; the page says pg_dump is generally not the right choice for regular production backups. | [PG 18 pg_dump](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/app-pgdump.html) | 2 | Repeats the pg_dump/RLS points in `postgres/best-practices.md` (internal). |
| F117 | Executed: as a non-bypass role `pg_dump` without `--enable-row-security` failed with "query would be affected by row-level security policy for table t01"; with `--enable-row-security --inserts` and the tenant id passed through `PGOPTIONS="-c app.tenant_id=..."` it wrote 100 of the 150 rows of `t01` (tenant A only); with no tenant context it wrote 0 rows of `t01`; a table **without** RLS (`tenants`, 100 rows) was dumped in full. | `run-s11b.sh`, `s11b-output.txt` (the S11 sub-test with the same purpose is a labelled wrong test, `s11-WRONG-TEST-note.txt`), 2026-10-16 | E | So an RLS-model tenant export is only as safe as the set of RLS-protected tables: any global table, and every table without a policy, is exported entirely. Export mechanics belong to another part. The "INSERT lines naming tenant B id: 1" in the output is the `tenants` row, not tenant data. |
| F118 | A base backup plus archived WAL restores an entire database cluster, "not a subset"; the stop point is chosen by time, named restore point or transaction id; recovery creates a new timeline; `pg_dump`/`pg_dumpall` cannot be part of the continuous-archiving solution. | [PG 18 continuous archiving and PITR](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/continuous-archiving.html) | 2 | Consequence `[A]`: in all three models on one cluster, point-in-time recovery of **one** tenant needs a whole-cluster restore into a side instance followed by logical extraction; only a separate cluster per tenant gives independent PITR. |
| F119 | `CREATE DATABASE ... TEMPLATE` copies the template (default strategy `WAL_LOG`, block by block, called efficient for small templates; `FILE_COPY` forces checkpoints before and after); no other session may be connected to the template while it is copied; `ALTER DATABASE` settings and grants are not copied from the template; `CONNECTION LIMIT` is enforced only approximately and not for superusers. | [PG 18 CREATE DATABASE](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-createdatabase.html) | 2 | Provisioning a tenant database needs the template quiet and per-database settings and grants applied after the copy. |
| F120 | Azure Architecture Center guide (page metadata updated 2026-10-11): a shared database gives the highest tenant density and lowest cost but carries noisy-neighbour and tenant-requirement limits; "restoring a single tenant might require you to restore the database to a separate resource" and recover selectively; dedicated databases isolate better and allow some customisation at higher cost, and need automated deployment; named antipatterns: per-tenant tables inside one database, manual schema changes, and an application tied to one schema version; row-level security is offered by some databases but is described as complex to design, implement, test and maintain; a mixed model (multitenant database for most tenants, single-tenant stamps for those who pay more or need more) is described. | [Azure Architecture Center: storage and data approaches](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/guide/multitenant/approaches/storage-data) | 4 | Vendor-neutral enough to corroborate, not a basis for decision (lead). It is written for Azure services in general, not PostgreSQL. |
| F121 | Executed, N = 100 tenants × T = 30 tables (each: UUID PK plus one `(tenant_id, c)` index), empty tables, same shape in all three models. **RLS** (one database, ENABLE+FORCE+policy on 30 tables, 100 rows in `tenants`): built in 0.35 s, `pg_database_size` 8,926 kB, 570 `pg_class` rows, 3,962 `pg_attribute` rows. **Schema-per-tenant** (one database, 100 schemas × 30 tables = 3,000 tables): provisioned in 11.28 s (0.113 s per tenant), 109 MB, 15,415 `pg_class` rows, 81,161 `pg_attribute` rows. **Database-per-tenant** (100 databases created from a 30-table template): provisioned in 10.20 s (0.102 s per database, 100 separate `psql` calls), template 8,774 kB, one tenant database 8,617 kB, the 100 together 841 MB, 565 `pg_class` rows inside each. | `run-s11.sh`, `s11-output.txt`, command `bash run-s11.sh 100 30`, `postgres:18.6`, 2026-10-16 | E | Marginal footprint per tenant `[A]`, from these numbers: about 1.0 MB (schema) and about 8.4 MB (database) against ~0 for RLS (rows only). Empty tables, one local container, single run, other load on the machine (S12 overlapped the end of S11); provisioning times include `docker exec` overhead and are not comparable between models to better than a rough order. Not measured: behaviour at N above 100, planner or relcache cost, autovacuum, memory per connection. Linear extrapolation to 1,000 tenants (≈150k `pg_class` rows, ≈1 GB for schemas; ≈8.4 GB for databases) is arithmetic, not a measurement. |
| F122 | Executed on the same setup: **schema change fan-out** (`ADD COLUMN` on every table): RLS 30 statements in 0.09 s; schema-per-tenant 3,000 statements in one transaction in 0.21 s; database-per-tenant 100 connections × 30 statements in 10.66 s. **Per-tenant dump** (`pg_dump -Fc`): `-n ten_50` of the schema database 0.35 s, 34,501 bytes; the whole database `tdb_50` 0.08 s, 34,001 bytes. **Restore of one tenant's schema** into a separate database with `pg_restore`: 0.25 s, 30 tables restored; the other tenants were not touched. A fresh-connection loop gave 50-58 ms for all three but is dominated by `docker exec`. | `run-s11.sh`, `s11-output.txt` | E | The schema-per-tenant figure is one transaction: one failure rolls back every tenant and nothing was measured about the locks held meanwhile (lock levels: NOT SOURCEABLE here, explicit-locking page not fetched). The database figure is linear in N `[A]`: about 107 s at 1,000 tenants before any real data, locking or failure handling. Connection-cost loop: inconclusive, not used. |
| F123 | `max_connections` is typically 100 by default (can be lower if kernel limits do), is set at server start only, and sizes shared resources, so raising it raises allocations; the page gives no per-connection memory figure. | [PG 18 runtime-config-connection](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/runtime-config-connection.html) | 2 | Combined with F113 `[A]`: a pooler holds a pool **per database and user pair**, so database-per-tenant multiplies pools by N (×roles); shared-schema keeps the pool count independent of N. The test container used 200, not the default. |
| F124 | The PostgreSQL 18 security page lists 46 entries; none affects 18.6 and none is fixed after 18.6 (highest fixed version listed: 18.6; 28 entries are fixed in 18.6, 11 in 18.4). Relevant: CVE-2026-14666 (row security caching disregards role modifications, fixed 18.6), CVE-2026-19385 (`pg_dump` heap buffer overflow, fixed 18.6, so dump/restore **client** tools must be at least 18.6 too), CVE-2026-14673 (amcheck untrusted search path, fixed 18.6), CVE-2026-14672 (`scram_iterations` user-existence oracle, fixed 18.6). | [PostgreSQL 18 security page](https://www.postgresql.org/support/security/18/) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 3 | Repeats restaurant-menu A34/A35 (their count of 28 fixed in 18.6 is reproduced). The page lists known vulnerabilities only. OSV: not queried, the OSV API is POST-only for the fetch tool (restaurant-menu A36 hit the same); GHSA has no PostgreSQL server record found. |
| F125 | OWASP ASVS 5.0.0, looked up in the raw v5.0.0 chapter files: **8.1.1** (L1) authorization documentation defines function-level and data-specific rules; **8.2.1** (L1) function-level access limited to explicit permissions; **8.2.2** (L1) data-specific access limited to consumers with explicit permission for those data items (IDOR/BOLA); **8.3.1** (L1) authorization enforced at a trusted service layer; **8.4.1** (L2) multi-tenant applications use cross-tenant controls so a consumer's operations never affect tenants they lack permission for (the only V8 requirement that names tenants); **8.4.2** (L3) administrative interfaces use layered security; **13.2.2** (L2) backend components, data layers included, run with least-privilege accounts; **13.2.3** (L2) no vendor-default credentials; **13.3.1** (L2) a secrets-management solution holds database integration credentials, none in source or build artifacts; **13.3.2** (L2) access to secrets is least-privilege; **13.3.4** (L3) secrets expire and rotate. | [V8 Authorization](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x17-V8-Authorization.md), [V13 Configuration](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x22-V13-Configuration.md) | 1 | Summaries come from the fetch tool, not verbatim; confirm wording before citing in the threat model. Logging IDs (V16) and 15.4.2 were not looked up here (not in this part's scope): NOT SOURCEABLE — fetch budget, not reached. |

#### 3.1 Go track — pgx 5.11.0 (area 1)

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F201 | pgx v5.11.0 was published 2026-09-07 (Go proxy `.info`); module `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5`. Matches the stack reference. | `ev/S20-pgx-source/` (`run.sh`, `output.txt`; `curl` of proxy.golang.org `…/@v/v5.11.0.info` and `.zip`, 2026-10-16) | 3 / E | All pgx source facts below were read line by line from the v5.11.0 module zip, not through a summariser. |
| F202 | **restaurant-menu B18/B19 CONFIRMED at v5.11.0.** `BeginFunc` (tx.go:402) and `BeginTxFunc` (tx.go:421) are package-level functions taking any value with `Begin`/`BeginTx`, so `pgx.BeginTxFunc(ctx, pool, opts, fn)` works. `beginFuncExec`: a deferred `Rollback` always runs (ignored only for `ErrTxClosed`); on `fn` error it rolls back and returns `fn`'s error; otherwise it returns `Commit`'s result. The deferred rollback also covers a panic in `fn`. | `ev/S20-pgx-source/output.txt` (tx.go excerpt) | 3 / E | Panic path also observed: F208. |
| F203 | **restaurant-menu B21 CONFIRMED.** `dbTx.Commit` marks the tx closed, and if the commit fails with server tx status not idle it closes the connection. `dbTx.Rollback` runs `rollback` through `conn.Exec`; if that fails (for example the context is already cancelled) it calls `conn.die()`. | `ev/S20-pgx-source/output.txt` | 3 / E | Observed effect: F209. |
| F204 | **restaurant-menu B22 CONFIRMED.** `pgxpool.Tx.Commit` and `.Rollback` call the inner tx and then release the connection. `pgxpool.Conn.Release` destroys the connection if it is closed, busy, or its tx status is not idle (`TxStatus() != 'I'`), or expired; otherwise, when `AfterRelease` is set it runs it in a goroutine and returns the connection to the pool only if the hook returns true. | `ev/S20-pgx-source/output.txt` (pgxpool/conn.go and tx.go excerpts) | 3 / E | A transaction left open at release cannot hand its local setting to the next borrower: F209. |
| F205 | **restaurant-menu B23 CONFIRMED.** Pool hooks at v5.11.0: `AfterConnect`, `BeforeAcquire` (marked Deprecated, "Use PrepareConn instead"), `PrepareConn(ctx, *pgx.Conn) (bool, error)` (false destroys the connection; a non-nil error fails the instigating query), `AfterRelease(*pgx.Conn) bool`, `BeforeClose`, `ShouldPing`. There is no `ResetSession` in `pgxpool`; `ResetSession` exists only on the `database/sql` adapter (`stdlib.OptionResetSession`, a no-op by default). | `ev/S20-pgx-source/output.txt` (pgxpool/pool.go, stdlib/sql.go excerpts) | 3 / E | `AfterRelease` takes no context and returns only a bool; a guard that must run a query belongs in `PrepareConn`. |
| F206 | pgx's own package documentation has a PgBouncer section: the default named-prepared-statement mode works with PgBouncer 1.21.0+ in transaction and statement pooling when `max_prepared_statements` > 0; otherwise set `DefaultQueryExecMode` to `QueryExecModeExec` (preferred), `QueryExecModeCacheDescribe` or `QueryExecModeSimpleProtocol`; do not use `QueryExecModeDescribeExec` with transaction pooling; SQL-level `PREPARE` is unsupported there. | `doc.go` in the v5.11.0 zip, `ev/S20-pgx-source/output.txt` (doc comment text; pkg.go.dev rendering not fetched) | 3 | Doc-comment text is the same source pkg.go.dev renders; not fetched separately. |
| F207 | **Forgotten context fails closed (Go).** As role `app_rt` (LOGIN, NOSUPERUSER, NOBYPASSRLS, not the owner; table ENABLE + FORCE RLS; policy with `NULLIF(current_setting('app.tenant_id', true), '')::uuid`): a plain pool query and a transaction with no `set_config` both saw 0 rows; an INSERT without context was rejected with SQLSTATE 42501 "new row violates row-level security policy". Test: `TestGo_ForgottenContextFailsClosed`. | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/` (`run.sh` `output.txt`, 2026-10-16, pgx 5.11.0, PG 18.6) | E | Consistent with restaurant-menu B25/B26 (PG-level semantics belong to another part). |
| F208 | **The local context does not outlive the transaction.** With a pool of size 1: after commit, after `fn` returning an error and after `fn` panicking (recovered by the caller), the next borrower got the same backend pid, 0 rows, and the setting reading `""`; the connection was idle and reusable. Test: `TestGo_LocalContextDoesNotSurviveCommitOrRollback`. | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt` | E | Confirms restaurant-menu B25 (empty string, not NULL, after a local set ends; fresh backend reads NULL, so the policy must keep `NULLIF`). |
| F209 | **Broken-connection paths destroy the connection.** (a) `fn` cancels the request context and returns an error: the deferred rollback fails, the next borrower is a different backend with no context. (b) A connection released while inside `begin … set_config(…, true)` is destroyed; next borrower is a different backend, 0 rows. Tests: `TestGo_CancelledContextAtRollbackDestroysConn`, `TestGo_LeakedOpenTxIsDestroyedOnRelease`. **The first run of these two FAILED because my assertion wrongly required `""`; the new backend reads `<NULL>`. Kept as `output-run1-WRONG-TEST-assertions.txt`; the final `output.txt` has the corrected assertions and all 15 tests pass.** | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output-run1-WRONG-TEST-assertions.txt`, `output.txt`, `README.md` | E | Agrees with F203/F204. |
| F210 | **Negative control.** `set_config('app.tenant_id', $1, false)` (session level) on a pooled connection is visible to the next borrower (3 rows of tenant A) both on a direct pool and through PgBouncer 1.26.0 in transaction mode across two independent clients sharing one server connection. Tests: `TestGo_SessionLevelSetConfigLeaks_NegativeControl`, `TestPooler_SessionLevelSetConfigLeaksAcrossClients_NegativeControl`. | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt` | E | Why `is_local = true` and a transaction per request are mandatory, and why a pool hook is not a substitute. |
| F211 | **A `PrepareConn` guard works as defence in depth.** A hook that queries `current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)` and checks tx status, returning false when the connection carries a value, destroyed the dirtied connection once and the next borrower was clean. Test: `TestGo_PrepareConnGuardCatchesLeakedSessionValue`. Cost is one extra round trip per acquire by construction; **not measured** (NOT SOURCEABLE — no timing run, turn budget). | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt` | E | |
| F212 | **restaurant-menu B26 CONFIRMED on the pgx path.** A second `set_config(…, true)` inside one transaction switches the tenant (3 rows became 5), so "one context per transaction" must be enforced by the Go helper (exactly one call, first statement). A malformed id raises 22P02 rather than returning 0 rows, so the Go layer should parse the id to a UUID type before binding. Tests: `TestGo_SecondSetConfigSwitchesTenant`, `TestGo_MalformedTenantRaises22P02`. | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt` | E | |
| F213 | **External transaction-mode pooler (PgBouncer 1.26.0, `default_pool_size=1`).** (a) `set_config(…, true)` inside a pgx transaction: a second client got the same server pid, 0 rows, empty setting (no leak). (b) pgx default mode (`cache_statement`) with `max_prepared_statements=200`: 60+ alternating-tenant transactions OK. (c) default mode with `max_prepared_statements=0`: first error `prepared statement "stmtcache_…" already exists (SQLSTATE 42P05)`. (d) `QueryExecModeExec` with `max_prepared_statements=0`: 40 alternating-tenant transactions OK. (e) `QueryExecModeDescribeExec`: 0 errors in 60 transactions, which does not prove safety; pgx documents it as unsafe (F206). | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt`, `pgbouncer-mps200.ini`, `pgbouncer-mps0.ini` | E | PgBouncer was reached over loopback, single connection pair; not a load test. |
| F214 | **Starvation fails fast, it does not borrow.** While a request holds the only connection inside an open transaction, the next acquire returned `context deadline exceeded`. Test: `TestAcquireTimeout_StarvationFailsFast`. | `ev/S21-pgx-runtime/output.txt` | E | Argues for an acquire timeout plus pool saturation metric. |
| F215 | PgBouncer's features page lists SET/RESET, LISTEN, WITH HOLD cursors, PREPARE/DEALLOCATE, PRESERVE/DELETE ROWS temp tables, LOAD and session-level advisory locks as never compatible with transaction pooling; protocol-level prepared statements work only with `max_prepared_statements` non-zero. The page does not mention `SET LOCAL` or transaction-scoped settings (same silence as in `docs/stack-reference/postgres/best-practices.md`); F213(a) is the evidence for that case. | [PgBouncer features](https://www.pgbouncer.org/features.html) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | The pgbouncer.org downloads page lists 1.26.1 (`ev/S22-maven-versions/output.txt`), newer than the 1.26.0 recorded in the stack reference. |
| F216 | testcontainers-go `@latest` is v0.44.0 (2026-08-07) for the root and `modules/postgres`, same as restaurant-menu B29; restaurant-menu B30–B34 (usage) not re-verified here. | `ev/S22-maven-versions/output.txt` (Go proxy) | 3 / E | Not pinned. |

| F234 | HikariCP 7.1.0 source: `ProxyConnection.close()` rolls back when the connection is in a dirty commit state and auto-commit is off, and resets only read-only, auto-commit, isolation, catalog, network timeout and schema (`PoolBase.resetConnectionState`). The source contains no `DISCARD`/`RESET ALL`, so a GUC set with `is_local=false` survives the return. `connectionInitSql` is only a config field used on new connections. | `ev/S23-jvm-source/output.txt` (sources jar `HikariCP-7.1.0-sources.jar`, 2026-10-16) | 3 / E | |
| F235 | **HikariCP behaviour observed.** (a) With auto-commit true, `set_config(…, true)` as its own statement is lost before the next statement: queries then see 0 rows, silently (fail closed but a "works in tests, empty in prod" bug). (b) A connection returned with an open transaction is rolled back and reused; the next borrower is clean. (c) Negative control: session-level `set_config(…, false)` survives the return; the next borrower sees tenant A. (d) A `DataSource` wrapper that checks the setting on `getConnection()` and calls `evictConnection` removed the dirty connection and handed out a clean one. | `ev/S24-jvm-runtime/output.txt` (K6a–K6d) | E | The wrapper is a proof of concept written for the spike, not a recommendation of that exact code. |
| F236 | pgjdbc 42.7.14 source: no `DISCARD ALL`/`RESET ALL` is issued by the driver on close; the only matches are places where the driver watches command tags for `DISCARD ALL`, `DEALLOCATE ALL`, `RESET` to flush its own caches. So the driver does not scrub GUCs either. | `ev/S23-jvm-source/output.txt` (QueryExecutorImpl excerpt) | 3 / E | |
| F237 | Exposed docs: `transaction()` runs synchronously on the current thread; `suspendTransaction()` in `exposed-jdbc` lets suspend functions run next to blocking JDBC; extra context goes through `withContext()`; a nested block shares its parent's transaction resources unless `db.useNestedTransactions = true` (savepoints). The page does not cover `exec()` raw SQL or statement hooks at transaction start. | [Exposed transactions](https://www.jetbrains.com/help/exposed/transactions.html) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | |
| F238 | Exposed 1.5.1 source: the current transaction lives in a `ThreadLocal` stack; `TransactionContextElement` is a `ThreadContextElement` that re-installs the transaction on whichever thread a coroutine resumes on; `suspendTransaction` is the supported entry point and `newSuspendedTransaction` is `@Deprecated` (WARNING level) in favour of it; the JDBC connection is created lazily on first use; a nested block with an outer transaction reuses the outer's connection; `Database.connect(datasource, setupConnection = …)` runs per new connection (not per transaction); `exec(sql, args) { rs -> … }` runs raw parameterised SQL. A `setupTxConnection` constructor parameter exists inside `TransactionManager`; whether it is a supported public hook is not established. | `ev/S23-jvm-source/output.txt`; `exposed-jdbc-1.5.1-sources.jar` read in `/tmp` and quoted by the script | 3 / E | Supersedes `ktor/best-practices.md` line 54 wording "inTopLevelSuspendTransaction": that function exists, but `suspendTransaction` is the documented path. |
| F239 | **Exposed observed behaviour.** Forgotten context: 0 rows and INSERT rejected with SQLSTATE 42501 (K1a/K1b). `suspendTransaction { exec set_config(…, true) … }`: tenant A sees 3 rows, the next borrower is clean on the same backend (K2a/K2b). Malformed id: 22P02 (K2c). A `withContext(Dispatchers.Default)` hop plus `delay()` inside `suspendTransaction`: the thread changed (`main` → `DefaultDispatcher-worker-1`) and the context still applied (3 rows), because it lives on the connection (K3). **Nested `suspendTransaction` with another tenant overwrites the outer context** (outer saw 5 rows after the inner block; K5). | `ev/S24-jvm-runtime/output.txt` | E | K3 queried through the explicit transaction receiver; implicit lookup was not separately tested. |
| F240 | **Coroutine / ThreadLocal pitfall.** The kotlinx.coroutines docs describe `ThreadContextElement` as the mechanism that installs thread-local state on each resume and restores it on suspension, and name `ThreadLocal.asContextElement` for using a `ThreadLocal` in a coroutine. Observed: a plain `ThreadLocal` tenant holder on a reused pool thread leaked request 1's tenant to request 2 (K4a); on a 4-thread dispatcher with `delay(1)`/`yield()`, 399 of 400 coroutines read a different value after resuming (K4b), versus 0 of 400 with `asContextElement` (K4c). | [ThreadContextElement](https://kotlinlang.org/api/kotlinx.coroutines/kotlinx-coroutines-core/kotlinx.coroutines/-thread-context-element/) (fetched 2026-10-17); `ev/S24-jvm-runtime/output.txt` | 2 / E | Design consequence: carry the tenant id as an explicit parameter or a context element, never a bare `ThreadLocal`/MDC; the database-side context is connection-bound and therefore not affected by thread hops (F239). |
| F241 | jOOQ licensing page: four editions (Open Source free; Express, Professional, Enterprise commercial); the runtime is Apache-2.0 and the FAQ says the open-source edition may be used in commercial software. **The page does not list which databases the open-source edition supports, so PostgreSQL coverage of the free edition is NOT SOURCEABLE from what I fetched** (I did not fetch a download or supported-databases page; turn budget). | [jOOQ licensing](https://www.jooq.org/legal/licensing) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | Do not assume "free for PostgreSQL" until `legal` / the owner confirms. Hosted-only product, so no redistribution question. |
| F242 | jOOQ docs: transactions are controlled by a `TransactionProvider`; the default implementation nests with JDBC savepoints; the page warns to use the `Configuration` passed to the callback, not the outer `DSLContext`. Observed on 3.21.9: `transactionResult { DSL.using(cfg) … set_config(…, true) … }` saw 3 rows; a forgotten context saw 0 rows; the next borrower was clean. | [jOOQ transaction management](https://www.jooq.org/doc/latest/manual/sql-execution/transaction-management/) (fetched 2026-10-17); `ev/S24-jvm-runtime/output.txt` (K7a–K7c) | 2 / E | Using the outer `DSLContext` inside the callback would bypass the transaction scope (docs warning); that is the jOOQ equivalent of "forgetting the context" and still fails closed by RLS. |
| F243 | Classpath observation: `exposed-core` 1.5.1 resolved `kotlin-reflect` 2.3.20 next to `kotlin-stdlib` 2.4.21 (the spike ran without a visible error). | `ev/S24-jvm-runtime/output.txt` (classpath line) | E | Minor; mention to the Gradle story. |

| F260 | **restaurant-menu C1/C3/C6 re-verified from docs.** Caddy `reverse_proxy` sets `X-Forwarded-For/-Proto/-Host` and ignores incoming values from untrusted clients; `header_up -Name` deletes a request header; a trailing-wildcard form (`-Some-*`) deletes matching headers; `header_up -*` is discouraged; `Forwarded` and `X-Real-IP` are not mentioned. | [Caddy reverse_proxy](https://caddyserver.com/docs/caddyfile/directives/reverse_proxy) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | |

| F262 | **restaurant-menu C2o/C4o/C6 re-verified on 2.11.7 against a neutral Go `net/http` echo upstream.** A plain `reverse_proxy` passed client-sent `X-Tenant-Id`, `X-Tenant-Slug`, `X-Tenant`, `X-Tenantdomain`, `X-Tenant-Foo`, `Forwarded`, `X-Real-Ip`, `X-Client-Ip`, `X-Forwarded-Prefix/-Port/-Server` unchanged, while `X-Forwarded-For` was replaced by the real peer, `X-Forwarded-Host` by the proxy's host and `X-Forwarded-Proto` by `http`. | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt` (section "Caddyfile.edge.plain"; `run.sh`, 2026-10-16, caddy:2.11.7-alpine) | E | |
| F263 | **`header_up -X-Tenant*` (no hyphen) closes the gap restaurant-menu S-C left open:** `X-Tenant-Id`, `X-Tenant-Slug`, `X-Tenant`, `X-Tenantdomain`, `X-Tenant-Foo` were all absent at the upstream; with `-Forwarded`, `-X-Real-IP` and explicit deletes of `X-Forwarded-Prefix/-Port/-Server`, none of those arrived either; `header_up X-Client-IP {client_ip}` replaced the client's value with the peer (`192.168.176.1`). | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt` ("CADDY 2.11.7: Caddyfile.edge") | E | Over-matching risk: the prefix also removes any legitimate header starting `X-Tenant`; choose header names once and reserve the prefix. |
| F264 | **Underscore header names.** Caddy 2.11.7 did not forward `X_Tenant_Id` even with a plain `reverse_proxy` and no `header_up` (absent at the Go echo upstream; the Caddy edge received it). The v2.11.7 source has a server option `expected_underscore_headers` and a comment (server.go:163–166) about headers with underscores and CGI/FastCGI ambiguity; **I read only the first lines of that block, so the default rule is established by the observation, not by the source.** Traefik 3.7.14 forwarded `X_tenant_id` by default (its entry point option `http.aliasHeadersStrategy` defaults to `keep`; `delete`/`reject` exist and apply to every header with a non-letter/digit/dash character). A Go `net/http` server received `X_tenant_id` as `X_tenant_id`. | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt`; `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output-run1-exploratory.txt` and `output-run2-goecho-without-plain-caddy-variant.txt` (earlier runs, see F277); `ev/S26-caddy-source/output.txt`; [Traefik entry points](https://doc.traefik.io/traefik/v3.7/reference/install-configuration/entrypoints/) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 3 / 2 / E | Ktor/Netty behaviour with underscore headers was not tested (PENDING — S27, not run). |

Traefik documents that it removes fields named in a request's `Connection` header early and offers `forwardedHeaders.connection` to exempt names. | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt` (R2 sections); [Traefik entry points](https://doc.traefik.io/traefik/v3.7/reference/install-configuration/entrypoints/) | E / 2 | Relevant to ASVS 4.1.3 (restaurant-menu C21): the intermediary-set header was not removable by the client in these two configurations. |

Traefik with `entryPoints.web.forwardedHeaders.trustedIPs` for the same ranges: `X-Forwarded-For: 6.6.6.6, 192.168.176.1` and a client `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` kept. Reconfirms restaurant-menu C10o. | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt` (trusted sections) | E | Trust only the actual front proxy's address, never a range containing users. |

Traefik offers no client-IP contract header like Caddy's `{client_ip}` in my config; the backend reads `X-Forwarded-For`, which for an untrusted edge contains one address. | `ev/S25-edge-proxies/output.txt` ("TRAEFIK 3.7.14: traefik-default.yml") | E | `X-Forwarded-Server` discloses an internal host name to the backend; the backend should ignore it. |

**3.1 Credential and enrolment standards (tier 1)**

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F301 | RFC 9421 (HTTP Message Signatures) leaves two things to each application: how a `keyid` resolves to key material (the text names preregistration as one way) and which message components must be covered. The verifier must decide whether the resolved key is trustworthy and reject unknown keys; it must also reject algorithm downgrades. | [RFC 9421 §1.4, §3.2, §3.2.1, §7.2.6, §7.3.4, §7.3.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9421.html) | 1 | Page read to character 200000 of 266070; the remainder (later §7.3 items, appendices) was not read. |
| F302 | RFC 9421 replay handling: the standard defines `created`, `expires` and `nonce` but sets no freshness window itself; `expires` is only a hint and a verifier may reject earlier. The application defines the acceptable age and may require unique nonces. A verifier must compare the `Content-Digest` header with the received body, otherwise the body can be swapped under a valid signature. A server can ask for a fresh signature with `Accept-Signature` plus a new `nonce`. Behind a reverse proxy the original request URI may be needed to verify `@target-uri`. | [RFC 9421 §1.4, §3.2.1, §7.2.2, §7.2.8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9421.html) | 1 | A signing scheme for Kodeebo must therefore pick the covered components, an age limit and a nonce rule itself; the standard does not. |
| F303 | RFC 9449 (DPoP): a DPoP proof shows possession of a private key. The proof is a JWT (`typ` `dpop+jwt`, asymmetric `alg`, public `jwk` in the header; claims `jti`, `htm`, `htu`, `iat`, plus `ath` when presenting an access token and `nonce` when the server supplied one). Each request needs its own proof. Servers accept proofs only inside a short `iat` window and may track `jti` values; strict single use across several servers may be infeasible. A server-issued `DPoP-Nonce` limits proofs pre-computed by an attacker who controls the client. | [RFC 9449 §4, §4.2, §4.3, §8, §11.1, §11.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9449.html) | 1 | Last 7022 characters (appendices) not read. |
| F304 | RFC 9449 scope: DPoP is written around an authorization server issuing access/refresh tokens and a resource server checking the key binding (`cnf` `jkt`); the proof does not cover the body or most headers, and DPoP "is not a substitute" for TLS (paraphrase of §2, §11.7). It is not client authentication by itself. Using it for Kodeebo would need a token endpoint and token lifetime management that a plain signed-request scheme does not. | [RFC 9449 §2, §3, §5, §6, §11.7](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9449.html) | 1 | The "needs a token endpoint" conclusion is an inference from the AS/RS model. |
| F305 | RFC 8705 (OAuth mutual TLS): a token can be bound to the SHA-256 thumbprint of the client certificate (`cnf` `x5t#S256`); the resource server compares it with the certificate from its TLS layer and answers 401 `invalid_token` on mismatch. A self-signed-certificate method exists in which the server does not validate a chain but matches the presented certificate against one registered for the client. How a client certificate reaches an application server behind a TLS-terminating proxy is out of scope of the RFC. Revocation checking is "possible" with the PKI method and left to deployment. | [RFC 8705 §2.1, §2.2, §3, §6.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8705.html) | 1 | The registered-self-signed-certificate model is the closest standard analogue of "enrol a device's own key, then match it". |
| F306 | RFC 8628 (device authorization): the user types a short `user_code` on a second device; the device holds a high-entropy `device_code`. `expires_in` bounds the code; `interval` (default 5 s) and `slow_down` / `expired_token` / `access_denied` govern polling. The user code has less entropy than the device code, so the server must rate-limit attempts and keep the lifetime short; the RFC's illustrative 8-character base-20 code is about 34.5 bits with roughly 5 attempts allowed per window. The server should show the code to be confirmed against the device screen and tell the user they are authorizing a device (§3.3.1, §5.4). | [RFC 8628 §3.2, §3.3.1, §3.5, §5.1, §5.2, §5.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8628.html) | 1 | RFC 8628 defines an OAuth grant, not a POS enrolment; used here as the standard pattern for "owner approves a code shown on a device". |

**3.2 OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 (tier 1; official v5.0.0 files, fetched 2026-10-17)**

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F307 | **V8 Authorization:** 8.1.1 (L1) document rules for function-level and data-specific access; 8.1.3 / 8.1.4 (L3) document the contextual attributes, including device, used in security decisions; 8.2.1 (L1) function access only with explicit permission; 8.2.2 (L1) data-item access only for permitted consumers (IDOR/BOLA); 8.3.1 (L1) authorization in a trusted service layer; 8.3.2 (L3) changes to authorization inputs apply immediately, or unauthorized actions are alerted and reverted; **8.4.1 (L2) in multi-tenant applications, cross-tenant controls so consumer operations never affect tenants they have no permission for**. 8.4.1 is the only tenant requirement in V8. | [ASVS v5.0.0 V8](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x17-V8-Authorization.md) | 1 | |
| F308 | **V12 Secure Communication:** 12.1.3 (L2) mTLS client certificates are validated as trusted before their identity is used for authentication or authorization; 12.1.4 (L3) certificate revocation checking enabled (for example OCSP stapling); 12.3.5 (L3) internal services authenticate each other strongly, for example TLS client authentication with PKI and replay-resistant methods; 12.3.4 (L2) with an internal CA or self-signed certificates, consumers trust only those specific certificates. | [ASVS v5.0.0 V12](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x21-V12-Secure-Communication.md) | 1 | |
| F309 | **V6 Authentication (enrolment-code and key relevant items):** 6.4.1 (L1) activation codes are random and expire quickly or after first use; 6.5.1 (L2) lookup secrets / out-of-band codes usable once; 6.5.3 (L2) generated with a CSPRNG; 6.5.4 (L2) at least 20 bits of entropy; 6.5.5 (L2) out-of-band lifetime at most 10 minutes; 6.6.2 (L2) a code is bound to its original request; 6.6.3 (L2) entry is rate-limited, "consider" 64 bits of entropy; 6.5.6 (L3) any factor can be revoked after theft or loss; 6.7.1 (L3) stored certificates that verify cryptographic assertions are protected from modification; 6.7.2 (L3) challenge nonces of at least 64 bits; 6.4.4 (L2) replacing a lost factor needs identity proofing at the level of the original enrolment; 6.3.7 (L3) notify on credential changes. | [ASVS v5.0.0 V6](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x15-V6-Authentication.md) | 1 | The ASVS floor (20 bits, 10 minutes) is far below the RFC 8628 example (about 34.5 bits, F306); both are stated, neither resolved here. These are written for human users; applying them to a device is an inference. |
| F310 | NOT SOURCEABLE — an ASVS 5.0.0 requirement that says "identity attributes such as tenant come from the authenticated credential, never from the request". Searched: the full V8 file (F307; the file's own closest items are 8.3.2 and 8.3.3), V6 (F309). V4 (API), V7 (session) and V9 (tokens beyond sync-note F323) were not read in this part. API Security Top 10 2023 API1 / API2 IDs were looked up in sync-note F324 and are not re-fetched. MASVS 2.1.0 does not apply (no mobile client in this part). | files above; sync-note F323, F324 | 1 | Cite 8.4.1 + 8.2.2 + 8.3.1 for the principle; do not cite a number for the "never from the payload" wording. |

Evidence directory: `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-3/` (README there lists every file, command, image digest and the two kept wrong runs). All E rows: PostgreSQL 18.6 (Debian 18.6-1.pgdg13+2), image `postgres:18.6`, own container `ti-p3-pg`, 2026-10-17. They show what this schema did on this version; they are not standards (tier E never overrides tier 1-2). No cryptography, no connection pool, no real runtime roles, no Kotlin driver was involved.

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F322 | **Identity from the credential, ids in the payload are only claims.** With the three identity settings (`app.tenant_id`, `app.branch_id`, `app.device_id`) set locally from the credential, `apply_cmd` filled the inbox row's `tenant_id`, `device_id` and `branch_id` from those settings only: a command with no ids applied, a command repeating the right ids applied, and commands claiming tenant B, another branch of tenant A, or another device of tenant A each returned `refused:id_mismatch` without touching the inbox. Each refusal wrote a `device_events` row under the **credential's** tenant, with the claimed ids kept as text in `detail`; tenant B had 0 events. | `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-3/10-tests.sql`, `10-tests.out` (T2 a-e, superuser view) | E | The function is a plain function in the database; the real signature check, the choice of where the settings are set, and the pool hand-off are not exercised. Claimed ids are attacker-controlled text stored in `detail`: bound their size and escape them when displayed. |
| F323 | **Duplicate, altered payload and used sequence number.** Replaying command 1 returned `duplicate:applied`; command 1 with a different body returned `flagged:payload_mismatch`; a new command id under used seq 2 returned `flagged:seq_conflict`. In both flagged cases the conflicting body was kept in `device_events.detail` and a `review_queue` row (`open`) was written, so nothing is dropped; a money fact carrying a review reason was applied and queued (`permission_revoked`). | same files (T2 f-i) | E | This implements the *proposal* that `payload_mismatch` / `seq_conflict` become record-and-flag (OQ6 still asks the founder). Under the unique `(tenant_id, device_id, device_seq)` the conflicting command cannot also be an inbox row, so it lives in the event and the queue. |
| F324 | **Missing context fails closed.** With no identity settings, `apply_cmd` raised `no device context (fail closed)`. With the settings unset, or set locally in an earlier committed transaction (reads back as an empty string), the device role saw 0 inbox rows and its insert into `device_events` failed the RLS check. | same files (T2 j, T6) | E | Agrees with restaurant-menu B25/B26 for the same PG minor. |
| F325 | **Pre-context lookup.** Credentials live in a table the device role cannot read (`permission denied for table device_credentials`). A `SECURITY DEFINER` function `auth_device(key_id)` with a pinned `search_path`, owned by a role that has an all-rows SELECT policy, returned the bound tenant, branch, device, device status and credential state for a known key and 0 rows for an unknown key; `EXECUTE` is granted to the device role only. | same files (T1) | E | This is the one global read of the design (key id has no tenant meaning). Same pattern as the sibling product's pre-context lookups (restaurant-menu A-part OQ2); only one function shape was tried. |
| F326 | **Composite keys catch what a lax policy lets through.** With a dashboard-role policy that checks the tenant only, an inbox insert for device dA1 with another branch of the same tenant, and one for another tenant's device, both failed on the composite foreign key `(tenant_id, device_id, branch_id) -> devices(tenant_id, id, branch_id)`. Device-role inserts of events/inbox with another tenant, another branch or another device failed the RLS `WITH CHECK`. An event with an unknown device cannot be stored in `device_events` (FK violation), so a refusal where **no** credential matched has no tenant to belong to. | same files (T4) | E | Matches sync-note F110 (FK checks bypass RLS) and F128. The FK error text echoes the attacker's own key values, which is not a leak across tenants. |
| F327 | **Wrong run, kept.** The first version of `apply_cmd` had no `SET search_path`, so as the device role it failed with `relation "inbox" does not exist`; T2 to T4 of that run proved nothing. A function that is not `SECURITY DEFINER` still runs with the caller's `search_path`; the fix is to pin it on the function (`SET search_path = s, pg_catalog`). | `00-setup.run1-wrong.sql`, `10-tests.run1-wrong.out` | E | Relevant to CVE-2026-14673-style search-path risks (F315). Keep the pin in every function the sync path calls. |
| F328 | **Who can read and write what (read/write matrix, one tenant-only dashboard policy).** Device role: SELECT own device row, INSERT into `inbox`, `device_events`, `offline_periods`, `review_queue` under a `WITH CHECK` on all three ids; cannot SELECT `review_queue` or `device_events`, cannot UPDATE `device_events`, cannot DELETE from `inbox` (permission denied). Dashboard role as tenant B saw 0 rows of tenant A and its UPDATE of a review item hit 0 rows; as tenant A it read 3 inbox, 5 event and 3 review rows and decided one item (`UPDATE 1`), but could not change `fact` or DELETE (permission denied). Ops role (explicit `USING (true)` policy, not BYPASSRLS) wrote the watermark of two tenants; a decrease of the watermark raised an exception (trigger); the device role read only its own watermark row and could not update it; a second device of the same tenant saw 0 rows; the dashboard role could not write it. | `10-tests.sql`, `10-tests.out` (T3, T5) | E | The role names are stand-ins; the real role design is another part's. Column-level UPDATE grants and `INSERT`-only grants did the append-only work, not RLS. |
| F329 | **Revocation versus an in-flight command.** (Case 1) A command path that only inserts the inbox row (the composite FK takes a KEY SHARE lock on the device row): a revoke issued 1 s later finished in about 0.06 s while the in-flight insert was still open, and the insert then committed about 2 s **after** the revoke, so the device's command was applied after revocation had committed. (Case 2) `apply_cmd`, which first reads the device row `FOR SHARE`: the revoke started 1 s later and finished at the same instant the in-flight command committed (blocked about 2 s); the next command was refused with `refused:device_not_active` and one `revoked_credential_used` event was written. | `20-revoke-race.sh`, `20-revoke-race.out` | E | Consistent with the documented lock matrix (restaurant-menu A21: KEY SHARE does not conflict with the lock an UPDATE of a non-key column takes). Two sessions, READ COMMITTED only. The `bc` utility was missing, so durations come from the printed times. |
| F330 | **A restore rewinds everything server-originated.** After a snapshot, a device was revoked, a new device and credential were enrolled, the owner accepted a review item, and a `clone_suspected` event was recorded. Restoring the snapshot into a fresh database brought back the revoked device as **active** (credential `active`), lost the new device and its credential, returned the review item to `open`, and dropped the event (count 0). RLS flags and all 18 policies came back with the dump; roles are cluster-level and were not part of it. | `30-restore-illustration.sh`, `30-restore-illustration.out` | E | A logical dump is not PITR; PITR also yields the state at the target and loses what came after (F314, tier 2). PITR run: PENDING - S33. Which of these facts a device can resend: none (they are server-originated). |
| F331 | PENDING - S31 (signed-request prototype on RFC 9421 or a hand-rolled scheme, with Go and Kotlin verifiers), PENDING - S32 (device TPM-backed key from each candidate desktop runtime: Compose Desktop / Electron / Tauri), PENDING - S33 (PITR with WAL archive and the watermark/revocation replay procedure), PENDING - S34 (concurrency beyond two sessions, pool hand-off of the three settings), PENDING - S35 (enrolment-code brute force and expiry under rate limits). None ran. | — | — | Spike ids S30-S39 are this part's block; S30 is the run above. |

### Part 4 — Branch scoping and session; encrypted local store binding and re-registration

Tier: 1 normative · 2 official docs · 3 official source/changelog/advisory · 4 engineering writing · 5 lead · E executed here (scripts and outputs in `docs/research/evidence/tenant-isolation/part-4/`) · "internal" = project file, not a source tier.

#### 3.1 Reused from parts 1-3 and other notes (not re-researched)

| # | Claim | Source | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|--------|------|-------|
| F401 | At PostgreSQL 18.6 a non-owner, non-bypass runtime role under ENABLE+FORCE row security sees only what its policies allow; a table owner or BYPASSRLS role does not; policies can be neither disabled nor changed by that runtime role; the catalogue queries C1/C2/C3/C8 find tables or views that lack the pattern. The branch policies of this part reuse that pattern unchanged. | part-1 F101-F104, F111 (executed, `postgres:18.6`) | E / 2 (reused, not re-run) | Part-1's sibling claims from restaurant-menu are not repeated here. |
| F402 | Tenant-context rules that carry over to a branch setting: unique keys and foreign keys on tenant tables lead with `tenant_id` (single-column FKs create cross-tenant references and existence oracles); views over tenant tables are `security_invoker`; a local setting reads as an empty string after its transaction, so policy casts need `NULLIF`; a second `set_config` in the same transaction silently switches the visible context; session-level `SET` leaks through a transaction-mode pooler while `SET LOCAL` does not. | part-1 F105, F106, F108, F110, F115 (executed) | E (reused) | F420 below shows the second-set problem applies to the branch setting too. |
| F403 | Device requests: `tenant_id`, `branch_id` and `device_id` come only from the authenticated credential, set as three transaction-local settings; ids in the payload are claims and a mismatch is refused with an event under the credential's tenant; the inbox has a composite FK `(tenant_id, device_id, branch_id) -> devices(...)`; missing context fails closed. | part-3 F322, F324, F326 (executed) | E (reused) | The branch setting of part-3 is a single `app.branch_id`; § 5 proposes one list-valued setting for all actors. |
| F404 | Key custody on Windows: DPAPI user scope separates other users of the machine but not the same logon or an administrator; machine scope lets any user decrypt; the DPAPI master key is derived from the logon credential (archived XP-era text, not re-verified on Windows 10/11); a TPM key can be non-exportable; options K1 user DPAPI, K2 machine DPAPI, K3 TPM, K4 server-escrowed, K5 PIN. SQLCipher encrypts every page with a per-page IV and HMAC, but not its temp files; a raw 256-bit key is passed as `x'<hex>'`; SQLite3MC's `PRAGMA key` reports success even with a wrong key, so a read must verify. | desktop-note F101, F104, F110, F112-F116, K1-K5 | 2 / 3 / 5 (as graded there) | Windows version floor, TPM presence on venue PCs, Platform provider algorithms: NOT SOURCEABLE there and in part-3 F313. |
| F405 | Local-store tamper expectations already written: TS-04 (copy of the store to another PC or Windows user must not open; no plaintext in database, WAL, journal, temp, dumps), TS-05 (a failed open must never be treated as a new install, never recreate or overwrite the file), TS-06 (file deleted: re-bootstrap from the server; the device identity must survive deletion or the device is re-enrolled with owner approval). OQ27: an administrator erasing offline sales that never synced is the open residual risk; founder position: the Windows administrator is only the owner or Kodelix's installer, a cashier is never an administrator. OQ28: rollback-journal mode is being weighed against WAL for acknowledged-sale durability. | sync-note OQ27, OQ28, TS-04, TS-05, TS-06 (`docs/research/local-first-sync.md`) | internal / E (as graded there) | A re-registration wipe is an administrator-level erase of a store that may hold unsynced sales: it is the same blind spot unless the drain gate of § 5 holds. |
| F406 | ETA receipts: each POS device has its own hash chain (`previousUUID` is the UUID of the previous receipt of the same POS; empty only for its first receipt); the POS must be registered and linked to the issuer RIN; a retired POS gets the whole submission rejected; the POS serial number is a mandatory field. A new device is a new chain. | eta-note F308, F315; part-3 F320 | 2 (reused) | Whether ETA lets a POS serial be re-linked to another issuer: not stated in the eta-note; the FAQ was re-read in F436 and is silent too. |
| F407 | Product facts: roles are owner, manager, cashier with branch assignment (item 5, W5); staff attendance is personal data under the PDPL (item 5, W6); store → branch → device, one cashier device per branch in the MVP, multi-device must not be blocked (item 7, P3, decision 2026-10-16); stock is per branch with transfers between branches (item 6, I1); daily order numbers per branch (O7); the owner Android app has the full dashboard and control, no selling; the manager "runs a shift or branch for the owner" (persona list). Anchor price is 10,000 EGP/year per venue; the value metric (per branch / station / tier) is open. | `design/product-brief.md` (items 5-7, decision log 2026-10-16, personas) | internal | Whether a manager may use the dashboard and the Android app is not stated: Q-OQ6 in § 9. |

#### 3.2 Per-branch scoping: standards and PostgreSQL documentation

| # | Claim | Source | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|--------|------|-------|
| F408 | `CREATE POLICY` (PG 18): policies are permissive by default; permissive policies of a command are ORed, restrictive ones ANDed with that result; with only restrictive policies no row is visible, so a permissive policy must grant first. `USING` hides rows silently on SELECT/UPDATE/DELETE; `WITH CHECK` fails the statement with an error on INSERT/UPDATE; for UPDATE and ALL, an omitted `WITH CHECK` falls back to `USING`. Policy expressions run with the invoking user's rights, may not contain aggregate or window functions, and are not applied to internal referential-integrity checks. The page gives no guidance on subqueries or performance. | [PG 18 CREATE POLICY](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-createpolicy.html) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | Basis for "tenant policy permissive + branch policy RESTRICTIVE" tested in F417-F421. |
| F409 | `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW` (PG 18): the page says nothing about row security, about whose privileges the defining query uses, or about who may read the view or refresh it; it states only that the query runs in a security-restricted operation with `search_path` temporarily `pg_catalog, pg_temp`. | [PG 18 CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-creatematerializedview.html) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 2 | Documentation silence is not evidence of safety; see F423 (executed: a materialized view cannot carry row security and leaked across tenants when its owner bypassed RLS). |

The file has **no requirement that sessions end when a role or permission changes** (the fetch tool said so; closest are 7.4.2, 7.4.3). | [ASVS v5.0.0 V7](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v5.0.0/5.0/en/0x16-V7-Session-Management.md) (fetched 2026-10-17) | 1 | Summaries come from the fetch tool, not verbatim; confirm wording before citing in the threat model.
