| B1-C | As B1-B but the scope is **derived in the database** from `staff_branches` and `app.staff_id` | Medium; no list handed over by the backend | Per-row SubPlan: 5.8-6.6 ms, 4,453 buffer hits in the same test (F424); hoisting not tried (PENDING - S42); the write policy that checks `app.actor_role` is only as good as the app that sets it (F421) | Slower than B1-B in the measured shape | Assignment change takes effect on the next transaction (F421), as it does with B1-B if the backend reads assignments per request |
| B1-D | Schema-level split: one schema or database per branch | High (part-1: per-tenant costs multiply by branches) | Cross-branch reports need fan-out; contradicts part-1's recommendation | n/a | Not pursued; listed because it is the structural alternative |

#### 4.2 B2 — how the dashboard / Android session carries scope

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| B2-A | **Opaque reference session token** (CSPRNG, at least 128 bits, ASVS 7.2.3); the backend resolves session → user → tenant → role → assigned branches from the database on each request and sets the settings | One session table, one lookup per request | The lookup is on every request path (cache decision in § 7) | One indexed point read plus assignments | Revocation is immediate: invalidate the row (7.4.1, 7.4.2); role/assignment change is picked up on the next request (F421); SPA stores no scope |
| B2-B | **Self-contained signed token** (JWT) carrying tenant, role and branch list, short lifetime | Key management, verifier, refresh flow | Stale scope until expiry or until a revocation list/cut-off is consulted (7.4.1); ASVS 9.1.x-9.2.x checks must all be right (F411); token size grows with branches | No per-request DB lookup for scope | Revocation needs the extra mechanism that B2-A has for free; ASVS has no rule ending sessions on role change (F410) |
| B2-C | Hybrid: short-lived signed token that carries only `sub`, `tenant_id`, `sid`; role and branch list always looked up server-side | Token plus lookup | Two mechanisms; the tenant claim is still a credential | Lookup remains | Gives stateless verification of identity only |

Android app: same API and the same token type; MASVS storage rules for holding it: NOT SOURCEABLE (F432).

#### 4.3 B3 — what happens to the local store when a PC is re-registered

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| B3-A | **Drain-then-crypto-erase**: refuse re-registration while the store has unsynced commands (server cursor equals local last sequence, confirmed by the server); then destroy the wrapped data key and the device credential, delete the files, create a new store with a new key under the new binding | Medium: a drain gate, an erase routine, an owner-visible "device was reset" event | Residual copies of the wrapped key (shadow copies, backups) keep the old store decryptable until the store files are also gone (F428, F404); unsynced sales are blocked by a gate that a broken PC cannot pass (needs the export path) | Off the counter path | Needs owner approval step (part-3 D1-A) and a clear UI; erase is local, offline-capable |
| B3-B | **Export-then-erase**: when drain is impossible, write an encrypted export of unsynced commands for the owner (or Kodelix installer) to import into the server inbox, then B3-A | Higher: export format, import path with tenant/branch/device checks, audit | The export file is a second copy of tenant-A data outside the store; an import path that accepts foreign tenant data is a cross-tenant write unless it checks the credential (part-3 F322) | One-off | Rare path; must be rehearsed |
| B3-C | **Overwrite/wipe only** (delete files, no key destruction), keep the same data key | Lowest | Free pages, WAL, journal, shadow copies keep plaintext-equivalent for anyone holding the key (F425, F426, F428) | none | Not acceptable for a cross-tenant move |
| B3-D | **Allow in-place re-binding** of the store to another tenant (relabel) | Low | Mixes two tenants' data in one file; defeats "one tenant per store"; rejected | none | n/a |

#### 4.4 B4 — binding the store to its tenant, branch and device

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| B4-A | Binding row **inside** the encrypted store (`store_meta`: tenant, branch, device, store id, schema version) compared at open with the credential's ids; mismatch = refuse to open, record event, never recreate (TS-05, F405) | Low | A row can be edited by someone holding the key (insider with the key) so it is a consistency check, not an authority | Negligible | Fits the existing TS-05 rule |
| B4-B | Additionally bind the **data-key wrap** to the same ids (DPAPI optional entropy or TPM key name containing tenant/branch/device ids), so a wrap made for tenant A cannot be unwrapped by the code path of tenant B | Medium | DPAPI entropy is a secondary secret that separates applications, not an access control (desktop-note F115); the claim that a TPM key name or authorization value gives a stronger bind is not sourced | Negligible | Key material and store are then lost/gained together |
| B4-C | Binding only in the file name or folder (`stores/<tenant>/<branch>/`) | Lowest | A path is not a binding; trivially copied or renamed | none | Useful as a convention only |

### Part 5 — Tenant export and deletion (PDPL, ETA retention); noisy-neighbour limits on sync

Licence check (L1-L4, T1): hosted only, so no customer bundle rule applies and **no option below is licence-blocked today**. If the product ever shipped a customer bundle, Redis 8.2 and Dragonfly (L1, L2) would be licence-blocked for it; Valkey 9.1.2 (L1) would not. `[A]` marks an inference that no fetched source states.

#### 4.1 Tenant deletion and the tax retention duty

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability (hosted-only) |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|---------------------------|
| DEL-1. Hard delete everything on request | Remove every row of the tenant, tax archive included, as soon as the owner asks. | Lowest build cost: row deletes in FK order (part-1 § 4 table). | Destroys the records the venue must keep five years after the tax period (eta-note F405, F415); the venue then holds no copy (eta-note D5: archive on our server only). Kodelix could be asked to restore them; counsel to say who is liable (F513). | One-off delete load. | Simple; irreversible. Conflicts with eta-note D5. |
| DEL-2. Staged closure: purge personal data now, keep the tax archive segregated for its period, backups expire | Owner-authenticated closure request, export window, then (a) delete staff personal data and customer PII not needed for tax, (b) keep the sales/receipt/ETA/cancelled-original records in a separate archive with its own `retain_until` (per-tenant policy, eta-note R3), de-identified wherever the tax record does not need the identity (F507, F513), restricted to export and legal-hold access, (c) delete the archive when the period ends, (d) backups age out inside the backup window (OQ29, F525) with deletions replayed after any restore. | Medium: archive split, `retain_until` and legal-hold fields, deletion log, restore-time tombstone replay. | Which fields a tax record needs to stay identifiable is not sourced (F513); a restore can resurrect deleted data unless tombstones are replayed (part-1 F118, part-3 control journal); an archive that outlives the contract is processing by Kodelix without an active customer: contract term for counsel. | Archive tables are append-only and cold; no hot-path effect. | Matches the Egyptian text's shape: erase when purpose ends, de-identify what is kept for a legal reason, log date and time of erasure (F507, F508). Needs a runbook and a deletion certificate. |
| DEL-3. DEL-2 plus per-tenant crypto-shredding of personal columns | Envelope-encrypt staff PII columns (names, phone numbers, attendance linkage) with a per-tenant data key held outside the database; deletion destroys the key, so copies in backups and WAL become unreadable before the backup expires. | High: key hierarchy, key backup lifetime matching the data, no SQL filtering or indexing on encrypted columns, rotation. | A destroyed key is only as good as every copy of the key: a key backup recreates the backup problem. Whether key destruction counts as "erase" under the Egyptian text is for counsel (F536). Backup-tool encryption cannot do this because it is per repository (F521). | Encrypt/decrypt per row on staff paths; not measured. | A second critical system to run; worth it only if counsel requires erasure from backups faster than the backup window, or the window cannot stay below 60 days. |
| DEL-4. Anonymise in place, keep every row | Overwrite staff names and identifiers with tokens, keep rows and keys. | Low-medium. | A scrubbed row set that can still be joined to the venue's staff list is still personal data; the backup copies keep the originals until they expire (F522 comparison). | None. | Useful as the mechanism for the de-identification step inside DEL-2, not as a stand-alone answer. |

#### 4.2 What the export contains and its format

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| EXP-1. `pg_dump` of the tenant (RLS mode, INSERT text) | As tested in part-1 F117. | Low. | Output is SQL for our schema, unusable for the owner; a table without RLS is dumped in full (part-1 F117) and would leak other tenants; needs the tenant in `PGOPTIONS`. | Whole-dump, not streamed per table. | Good for our own restore of a tenant, not for the owner. |
| EXP-2. Per-table `COPY (SELECT ... WHERE tenant_id = ...) TO STDOUT` as CSV with header, plus byte-exact JSON for signed payloads, a manifest and a data dictionary, zipped | Runs as the non-bypass runtime role with the tenant context set, so RLS filters (F530); one file per table; ETA receipt/invoice payloads exported as the stored JSON/XML byte-for-byte (eta-note R3 hash manifest); manifest lists file, row count, SHA-256, export time, schema version; passcode hashes and device keys excluded. `[A]` for the contents list beyond eta-note R3. | Medium: table inventory per release, a test that every tenant table is covered (part-1 C-queries). | Missing a table silently; huge exports (long archives) need streaming and a time limit; an export is a PII exfiltration path (abuse case 2). | Streams without building the whole file in memory (COPY TO STDOUT); load on the primary: run on a replica or in a low-priority class `[A]`. | Open formats the owner's accountant can read. No format is prescribed by the Egyptian text read (F505, F508). |
| EXP-3. Paginated JSON API export | Owner dashboard pulls pages. | Medium-high: pagination, consistency across pages. | Inconsistent snapshots; slow for years of data; per-request limits (F528) interact. | Many round trips. | Fits dashboards, not a closure archive. |

#### 4.3 Where limits are enforced

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| RL-A. Edge only (Traefik `RateLimit` or Caddy module) | Token bucket per IP or header at the proxy (F520, F531). | Lowest. | Identity unknown before the credential is verified (part-3 D3-A), so a tenant or device key cannot be trusted at the edge; NAT'd cafes share IPs; Traefik's rejection response is not documented (F520); Caddy needs a custom build (F531). | Very cheap, before the app. | Fine as a coarse pre-auth guard and body-size cap; cannot enforce per-plan quotas. |
| RL-B. In-process only (`x/time/rate` or JVM equivalent) | Token bucket in each backend instance keyed by `(tenant, device)` after verification (F532). | Low. | Per-instance: N instances give N times the limit; state lost on restart; JVM library not researched. | No network hop. | Good first line and fallback when the shared store is down. |
| RL-C. App-level, shared counter in Valkey (Lua token bucket or GCRA), edge coarse guard, in-process fallback | Script keyed `rl:{tenant}:...` (F519, F529); algorithm in the script (F524); `EVAL` restricted to the limiter's account (F533). | Medium: script, tests, limits cache, metrics. | The limiter is a new dependency: fail-open to RL-B on error so sync never stops because the limiter is down `[A]`; Lua attack surface (F533); GCRA is not a built-in (F519). | One Redis-protocol round trip per sync request: not measured, PENDING — S51. | Matches the repo's existing Valkey/Redis use and `redis/best-practices.md` § 5; Dragonfly/Redis scripting parity not checked. |
| RL-D. Counters in PostgreSQL | Update a quota row per request/day. | Medium. | Write amplification on the hot path and row contention per tenant; competes with the very resource being protected. | Worst of the four for request-rate limits. | Reasonable only for slow quotas (daily bytes, export counts), not per-request. |

#### 4.4 Algorithm

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| ALG-1. Fixed window counter | `INCR`+`EXPIRE` in one script (F519). | Lowest. | Edge-of-window double burst; a long-outage catch-up hits the wall at window start `[A]`. | One key per window. | Easy to explain; poor burst semantics. |
| ALG-2. Token bucket | Refill `r`/s, capacity `b` (F520, F532). | Low. | Needs two stored values (tokens, timestamp) in a shared store. | Good. | The capacity `b` is the catch-up burst allowance: a data field per plan. |
| ALG-3. GCRA | One stored TAT per key (F524). | Medium (own script, tested). | Not built into Valkey/Redis (F519); one tier-4 source. | One value per key. | Equivalent behaviour to a token bucket with less state. |

### Part 6 — Cross-tenant negative test catalogue; ASVS / MASVS / API Top 10 mapping

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability (hosted; no self-hosted bundle) |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------------------------------------|
| A | **Generated isolation matrix + story-specific tests.** A shared suite derived from `contracts/` (object types × routes × principals) run in CI, plus hand-written SY/AN/DB tests per story; DB catalogue test (DB-01..06) in CI | Highest to build once; low marginal cost per story | Matrix only covers routes in the contract; undocumented routes need an inventory check (ASVS/API9) | Matrix and Testcontainers run add CI minutes; split into a per-PR subset and a nightly full run | One owner for the matrix (qa-lead); fails the build when a route has no case |
| B | **Per-story hand-written tests only** (security-baseline default) | Low up front | Cases forgotten for new routes; API1 page says tests must gate deploy | Cheapest per run | Depends on reviewer discipline |
| C | **Add a black-box scanner or pen-test pass** on top of A | Tool and tester cost; no source read on tools here (NOT SOURCEABLE) | False sense of completeness; cannot see device-signed sync | Runs outside CI | Needs a staging environment with seeded tenants |
| D (Android) | Instrumented emulator tests (AN-02/03/05/06) vs static-only checks | D-instrumented: emulator in CI, slower; D-static: cheap | Static checks miss runtime caches (AN-03) | Instrumented adds minutes | Manual MASTG passes remain a release step either way |
| E (DB) | Catalogue + runtime-role tests (DB-xx) vs app-layer tests alone | E: small, runs in seconds on the probe (S60) | App-only misses a table added without RLS (F621) | negligible | Needs a Postgres container in the test environment (Testcontainers unpinned; part-2) |

No option here breaks L1-L4 or T1: every tool is test-time only and the product is hosted-only. Testcontainers' licence was not checked (NOT SOURCEABLE — not fetched).

## 5. Decision

**Decided by the user on 2026-10-17** as the input to the ADRs; the `cto` records the architectural ones (tenancy model, tenant context, device credential, server sync state, branch scope and session, retention/deletion, limits). Counsel decides every legal reading. Verdict `BLOCKED` (open questions block implementation, § 9.1), so Status stays `Draft`.

- **Chosen (user, 2026-10-17)**: the whole recommendation set in § 4.1 goes forward as the input to the `cto` ADRs, flags kept — tenancy A (shared-schema-rls) built so D stays possible; G1 / J1 tenant helper and P1; either proxy plus X3; D1-A + D2-B, D3-A, D4-A, D5-C; B1-B + B2-A, B4-A+B, B3-A; DEL-2 + EXP-2; RL-C (limits delay, never drop); tests A + E. **Flagged, not accepted until their spikes report**: D2-B (S31, S32), D5-C (S33), B4-B and B3-A (S41, S43).
- **Tenant unit (founder, 2026-10-17; to be confirmed in the tenancy ADR)**: tenant = the owner organisation; branch = one venue; device = one cashier PC. The licence and the price are counted per cashier device, not per branch; the owner dashboard and the owner Android app are not billable devices. In Tier 1 a branch has one cashier device, so the device count equals the branch count until multi-device sync per branch exists. Replacing a device moves its licence and must not consume a new one. Consequence for the design (inference): the licence seat is a record separate from `device_id`; re-registration still creates a new `device_id` (part-3 D4-A, part-4 B3-A), retires the old one and moves the seat to it in the same owner-approved step — feeds P3-OQ1, P3-OQ7, Q-OQ4, Q-OQ5 and `/license-design`.
- **Licence issuance (founder, 2026-10-17)**: seats come only from Kodelix. The operator sets the number of cashier-device seats per tenant from the contract, as a licence signed by Kodelix. The owner can enrol devices (D1-A) only within those seats; a device beyond them needs a new seat from Kodelix. Kodelix is notified of every activation and every seat move. **D1-A is amended accordingly: the owner's enrolment code is valid only while a free seat exists** (checked when the code is created and again when it is redeemed).
- **Device binding (founder requirement, for `/license-design` and the credential ADR; not decided here)**: the licence must be bound to the device — hardware and software — and to its install. To research there: (a) best practice for the binding — a non-exportable TPM/DPAPI-held key (D2-B, S32) versus a hardware/software fingerprint versus both (fingerprint as a clone/change signal), including false lockouts after hardware changes or Windows updates; (b) whether every activation needs Kodelix approval or seat moves get a yearly allowance set by the founder; (c) how the N5 offline grace bounds an old device that keeps selling offline after its seat has moved.
- **Gaps (user)**: each NOT SOURCEABLE or PENDING gap blocks only the ADR it feeds — counsel items (PDPL roles, licence and DPO; erasure vs backups vs ETA archive; key destruction as erasure; ETA re-registration) → the retention/deletion ADR and the re-registration flow; S31/S32 → the credential ADR; S33 → the storage ADR; S41/S43 → the key-custody ADR. None blocks the tenancy ADR or the sync spike. (The counsel items in this list are now answered by founder decision, not counsel — see the next bullet; ETA re-registration stays open.)
- **Founder decisions in place of counsel answers (2026-10-17) — answered by founder decision, not counsel.** Source: founder statement, until the founder adds counsel's written answer and the licence numbers. Brief: `docs/architecture/privacy/pdpl-counsel-brief-2026-10.md`.
  1. **Hosting and licences (brief Q6, Q2):** counsel cleared hosting outside Egypt, and the required PDPL licences have been obtained.
  2. **Roles (Q1):** the venue owner is the controller of staff data; Kodelix is the processor and acts on the owner's written instruction.
  3. **Staff records (Q5):** they belong to the owner; Labour Law retention is the owner's duty. Kodelix gives the owner the export (EXP-2) and then deletes.
  4. **Deletion (Q4):** DEL-2 as recommended — staged closure, purge personal data, tax archive kept segregated for its period, backups expire inside the window, deletions replayed after any restore; EXP-2 for export; DEL-3 stays the escalation.
  5. **Key destruction counts as erasure.** This closes the erasure condition behind B3-A; spikes S41 and S43 still have to run, so B3-A stays flagged on them.
  6. **No staff consent screen for storage abroad** in the current release.
- **Why**: three venues at 10,000 EGP/yr make per-tenant fixed cost decisive (F121-F123); every mechanism the set relies on ran at 18.6 (F103-F115, F207-F242, F322-F330, F417-F423, F617-F621); the set is consistent across parts — one scope helper, one control journal, no authorization cache (§ 4.2 items 2, 3, 6).
- **Why not the others**: schema- or db-per-tenant pay linear cost and migration fan-out for three venues with no customer asking for physical isolation (reopen on § 9.1 item 4); mTLS adds a CA and certificate renewal that an offline Windows POS can miss; a bearer token makes a copied disk the device; app-only branch filtering is one forgotten WHERE away from a sibling branch (F418); hard delete destroys the venue's only tax copy (eta-note D5); edge-only limits cannot see the tenant before the device signature is verified (F520).
- **Follow-up record**: ADRs via `/architecture-decision` — tenancy family (model, tenant context, branch scope and session; confirms the tenant unit); device credential and enrolment; server sync state and restore (with the sync write-path ADR); key custody and re-registration; retention, deletion and export; sync limits and the limiter dependency. Threat models `tenant-isolation` and `local-first-sync`. Counsel brief before 2026-11-01. `/license-design`: seats per cashier device, the seat moves on replacement. Spikes in § 9.2. CTO-STACK-RISK skipped - lean mode. No ADR exists yet; `docs/architecture/` is absent in this repo.

### Part 1 — PostgreSQL RLS re-verification at 18.6; tenancy model options

provisional until the caller records the choice.

**Chosen**: Recommended - pending user decision: **Option A (`shared-schema-rls`) for the MVP**, built so that **Option D** stays possible: all tenant-owned data is reached through a tenant-to-connection resolver port, so one tenant can later be moved to its own database without touching callers. The `cto` decides by ADR.

**Why**:
- Three venues and unknown growth at 10,000 EGP/year each make per-tenant fixed cost matter: A adds ~0 MB per tenant and one migration target (F121, F122), while B adds about 1 MB and 150 catalogue relations per tenant and C about 8.4 MB, a pool partition and an N-way migration (F121-F123).
- The PostgreSQL mechanisms A needs all held at 18.6 under test: ENABLE+FORCE filtering the owner, non-bypass runtime role without ownership unable to change policies, transaction-local context safe behind a transaction-mode pooler (PgBouncer 1.26.0) when `set_config(..., true)` or `SET LOCAL` is used inside an explicit transaction (F101-F104, F110, F115).
- A's weaknesses are known and testable rather than unknown: session-level settings leak through a pooler (F115), single-column FKs and non-tenant-leading unique keys leak existence or accept cross-tenant references (F105, F106), views owned by a bypass role leak (F108), a second `set_config` silently switches tenant (F110). Each has a rule and a check (catalogue queries F111; Go-side single set; composite FKs).
- It matches the product's default stance (`security-baseline.md` names RLS first, `migrations.md` requires `tenant_id` and an RLS policy per `tenancy.model`) and the sibling note's conclusion, but this note's evidence is its own (S10-S12), not an inheritance.

**Why not the others**:
- B (schema-per-tenant) pays the fan-out and catalogue growth of C without C's structural boundary, depends on `search_path` behaviour behind a pooler that PgBouncer only began tracking by default in 1.26.0 and still lists bugs for (F114), and Azure's guide lists per-tenant tables in a single database as an antipattern (F120, tier 4 lead). Its one real strength: cheap logical export and restore of one tenant (0.35 s dump, 0.25 s restore, F122).
- C (db-per-tenant) has the strongest boundary and the easiest delete and single-tenant logical restore, but the cost is linear: 841 MB for 100 empty tenants, a 10.66 s migration for 100 databases, a pool per database and user against a default of about 100 connections (F121-F123), and no independent PITR (F118). Right when a tenant requires it, which is Option D.
- D is the exit hatch, not the starting point: it doubles the code paths before any tenant needs the second one.

**Conditions that should reopen the choice** (thresholds are the cto's to set; none is sourced): a signed customer or regulator requirement for physical isolation, own keys or data location (Open question 1); a per-tenant restore or erasure demand frequent enough that row-level surgery (A) is the dominant operations cost (question 5); measured noisy-neighbour impact; tenant count or data volume beyond what the first load test supports (question 3).

**Follow-up record**: ADR on the tenancy model (`cto`), recorded before `tenancy.model` is set in `project.yaml`; `/threat-model tenant-isolation`; a note to this part when S13/S14 run.

##### Proposed pin changes (the user reviews and applies; this note edits no pin)

| Key | Old value | New value | Source | Date | VERSION.md it derives from |
|-----|-----------|-----------|--------|------|-----------------------------|
| connection pooler (key name for the user to choose, e.g. a `pgbouncer` entry) | not pinned | `1.26.1` **only if** a transaction-mode pooler is adopted (Open question 4); never below 1.26.1 | [PgBouncer changelog](https://www.pgbouncer.org/changelog.html) (1.26.1, 2026-10-14, F114) | 2026-10-17 | none yet (no stack reference for the pooler; researcher can draft one on request) |

Stack-reference follow-up (not applied): `docs/stack-reference/postgres/best-practices.md` names PgBouncer 1.26.0 (2026-09-23) as the latest release and its three CVEs; 1.26.1 (2026-10-14) fixes three more (F114), so that line is one release old.

### Part 2 — Tenant context per backend track (pgx; pgjdbc, HikariCP, Exposed, jOOQ); edge proxy (Caddy, Traefik)

**Recommended - pending user decision** (the `cto` records the final choice; ADR needed: yes, together with the tenancy-model ADR).

- Go track: **G1**, with the G2 guard as an optional alarm, not as the control. Tenant context is `set_config(…, true)` inside a transaction opened by a single helper, parsed UUID, exactly one call, first statement; repositories take the helper's tx type. Backed by F207–F214.
- JVM track: **J1** (Exposed `suspendTransaction`), tenant id passed explicitly or via `asContextElement`, never a bare `ThreadLocal`; ban nested tenant transaction blocks; keep HikariCP `autoCommit` default only if the code always runs inside Exposed transactions. **J2 (jOOQ)** stays an alternative pending the licence confirmation of F241. Backed by F232–F242.
- Pooler: P1 until load requires P2. If P2 is chosen, use PgBouncer with `max_prepared_statements` > 0 (pgx default mode, pgjdbc defaults) or switch pgx to `QueryExecModeExec` / pgjdbc to `prepareThreshold=0` (F213, F232). The transaction-local setting itself is safe behind the pooler (F213a).
- Proxy: both are viable. If the product wants a single deny-rule for a tenant header family, Caddy is easier (F263 vs F269); if Traefik is chosen, name every tenant-related header explicitly and set `aliasHeadersStrategy`. In both cases add X3 (backend never trusts a tenant header) and keep `trusted_proxies` / `forwardedHeaders.trustedIPs` empty unless a front proxy exists (F266). Tie the proxy choice to the infra decision, not to this note.

Why the others lost: G3 and J4 leak (F210, F240); J3 duplicates what Exposed/jOOQ provide; P2 is not needed until a connection limit is shown (no load data in this part).

Follow-up record: ADR (tenant context mechanism, with the tenancy model); ADR "client IP source" with the edge decision.

### Part 3 — Device enrolment and credential; sync request scoping; server-side sync state

**Chosen**: **Recommended - pending user decision** (the `cto` decides by ADR; the founder decides the OQ6 questions below).

1. **Enrolment: D1-A** — owner-created, branch-bound, single-use, expiring code, followed by owner approval comparing a key fingerprint. The branch comes from the authenticated owner session that created the code, never from the device. Numeric values (code length, lifetime, attempt cap) are configuration, to be set by `security-lead` inside the floors of F309 (at least 20 bits and at most 10 minutes for out-of-band codes; "consider" 64 bits) and the RFC 8628 example (about 34.5 bits with about 5 attempts per window, F306); this note does not choose a number.
2. **Credential: D2-B (RFC 9421 signed requests) with a device-generated key pair**, as the working recommendation, on condition that spike S31 shows a Go **and** Kotlin verifier with a server-supplied nonce is feasible and S32 shows each candidate desktop runtime can sign with a key held in DPAPI (K1/K2) or, where a TPM exists, the TPM (K3). If S32 fails for the chosen runtime, fall back to D2-A (mTLS) for the proxy-terminated path only if F312 gets closed, otherwise D2-D (bearer, hash-only on the server) with K1 wrapping as an explicitly accepted weaker tier. DPoP (D2-C) is not recommended: it needs a token endpoint and token lifetimes that a POS does not otherwise need (F304).
3. **Request scoping: D3-A** — `tenant_id`, `branch_id`, `device_id` come only from the credential, resolved by one narrow pre-context lookup (F325) and handed to the transaction as three local settings (F322, F324); the request schema carries no id fields; an id that is present and differs is refused with an event under the credential's tenant (F322). The inbox key `(tenant_id, device_id, command_id)` and the unique `(tenant_id, device_id, device_seq)` are filled from those settings, not from the body. Composite foreign keys `(tenant_id, device_id, branch_id)` stay as the database backstop (F326). Every function on this path pins `search_path` (F327).
4. **Revocation and rotation: D4-A** — status on the device row, read `FOR SHARE` on every command path so a revoke orders against an in-flight command (F329); rotation by a staged key signed with the old key; **re-registration of a replaced or reinstalled PC is a new `device_id`** with its own sequence, approved through D1-A again (ASVS 6.4.4, F309). No status cache at Tier 1 (§ 7).
5. **State: D5-C** with the table plan in § 4.6 — main database for all tables, plus an append-only control journal outside the restored database for the facts a device cannot resend (enrolment, approval, revocation, rotation, owner review decisions, security events), replayed by the restore runbook; PITR to the latest possible point stays the default restore. `verified_restore_watermark` is written only by the backup-verification job, is monotonic, and takes its authority from the backup catalogue.
6. **Unknown or invalid credentials** (no tenant derivable) are recorded outside tenant tables in a platform sink and counted; the owner is alerted only for refusals that carry a credential which resolves to a tenant, and those alerts are aggregated and rate-limited (P3-OQ2).

**Why**: only D1-A makes the binding an act of an authenticated owner, with the approval step as the defence against a leaked code; a device-generated key pair means the server holds nothing that impersonates the device (F305 registered-certificate model); D2-B needs no CA and works with revocation as a row update, but it is the option whose profile we must write ourselves (F301, F302), which is why S31 and S32 are conditions, not formalities. D3-A is the only variant that satisfies the founder's OQ6 wording (a forged id is a security event and an owner alert) and it was exercised on 18.6 (F322-F326). D4-A rests on an executed ordering difference (F329). D5-C follows from F330: PostgreSQL gives a consistent snapshot at the target and nothing after it (F314), so facts only the server holds need a second copy.
**Why not the others**: D1-B invites unauthenticated queue spam and owner mis-approval; D1-C breaks "key generated on the device"; D1-D is not a device binding. D2-A is a sound standard (F305, F308) but adds a CA, renewal that offline devices can miss, and an unsourced proxy hop (F312); it stays the fallback. D2-D is simplest but a copied disk or leaked log is full impersonation. D3-B cannot raise the required security event; D3-C is the cross-tenant-write class ASVS 8.4.1 targets. D4-B and D4-C trade revocation latency for convenience; ASVS 8.3.2 asks for immediate effect or alert-and-revert (F307). D5-A loses revocations and owner decisions (F330); D5-B breaks the composite keys that F326 relies on.
**Follow-up record**: ADR needed — yes: (a) device credential and enrolment (feeds the key-custody ADR and the N5 lease ADR), (b) server-side sync state and restore procedure (feeds the sync write-path ADR and the backup runbook), both under the `cto` tenancy ADR. Threat model: `docs/architecture/threat-models/local-first-sync.md` (proposed in the sync note, not yet written) must include the abuse cases in § 6. The conditions S31, S32, S33 are PENDING: a decision resting on them is flagged, not accepted.

#### Proposed pin changes

None. No new version is adopted here; the RFC 9421 library question is open (P3-OQ5) and `pins.yaml` is untouched.

### Part 4 — Branch scoping and session; encrypted local store binding and re-registration

**Chosen**: **Recommended - pending user decision** (the `cto` decides by ADR; the founder decides the role questions in § 9). Decisions 4-6 rest on spikes that did not run (S41, S43): flagged, not accepted.

1. **Data model** (design proposal built on the tested schema, F417-F422): `tenants` → `branches (tenant_id, id)` → `devices (tenant_id, branch_id, id)` with composite FKs at every level; `staff (tenant_id, id, role ∈ owner|manager|cashier)`; `staff_branches (tenant_id, staff_id, branch_id)` for manager and cashier assignment (an owner has every branch by role, not by rows). Every branch-scoped fact table carries `tenant_id` **and** `branch_id`, leads indexes with `tenant_id, branch_id`, and references the branch by a composite FK (F418). Tenant-wide data (catalogue, staff list) is not branch-scoped; transfers between branches (brief I1) touch two branches and need an owner or a role that holds both.
2. **Enforcement: B1-B, both layers.** RLS carries tenant (permissive) and branch (restrictive) policies on one list-valued setting (`app.branch_ids`, or `app.branch_all='on'` for the owner role), set once, first, by one helper that rejects a second call (F420); the application layer owns function-level permissions (owner/manager/cashier, API5, F412) and the derivation of the list. Device requests use the same setting with a one-element list taken from the credential (part-3 F322/F403), so there is one policy shape for people and devices. B1-C is the fallback if the backend should not hand lists to the database, at a measured 2x cost in the small test (F424, S42 pending). Never use a client-sent branch id as scope: if a request names a branch, it is checked to be inside the server-derived set, else refused (F418).
3. **Session: B2-A** (opaque reference token, scope resolved per request from the database), because it makes role and assignment changes effective on the next request (F421) where ASVS gives no rule for it (F410) and a self-contained token would need a revocation list or cut-off (7.4.1). B2-C if per-request lookup proves too costly. The owner Android app uses the same token; its storage rules need MASVS text (F432).
4. **Reports:** owner cross-branch totals and manager branch totals are one `security_invoker` view or query running under the caller's own scope (F422); pre-aggregated rollups are ordinary tables with `tenant_id`, `branch_id`, ENABLE+FORCE and the same policies, never materialized views (F423).
5. **Local store: one store per (tenant, branch, device); B4-A plus B4-B.** At open the app compares the store's binding with the credential and refuses on any mismatch without touching the file (F405). Which key custody tier wraps the data key is the key-custody ADR's choice; this note only requires that the wrap be tied to the same ids.
6. **Re-registration: B3-A, with B3-B as the exception path.** Same PC to another tenant or branch = a new `device_id` (part-3 §5.4) and a new store; the old store is crypto-erased only after the server confirms the drain, or after an owner-acknowledged export. Erase order: stop the app, confirm drain, destroy the wrapped key and device credential, then delete store, WAL and journal files, then (best effort) empty free space. Because VSS keeps old blocks (F428) and SQLite keeps stale pages (F425, F426), the property relied on is that the key is gone, which is a real erase only if no copy of the wrapped key or DEK survives: a TPM-held key makes this credible even if a blob copy exists (`[A]`, F429 silent), DPAPI user scope does not (blob copies plus the user's master key, desktop-note F112/F115). The owner is told "device reset" with the tenant, branch and time (OQ27 condition 2 in spirit).
7. **ETA:** re-registration to another tenant creates a different issuer and therefore a different POS and chain; the old chain ends where the old store ended. Same-tenant re-registration (replacement PC) needs the chain's last `previousUUID`, which only the server can supply if every receipt reached it (eta-note F308, F436). Everything beyond that is NOT SOURCEABLE (Q-OQ4).

**Why**: RLS plus application checks is what the tests show working (F417-F421) and what ASVS 8.2.2/8.3.1/8.4.1 ask for in spirit; a reference token turns assignment changes into next-request effects without extra machinery; one policy shape serves staff and devices; crypto-erase is the only erase that does not depend on what the file system leaves behind (F425-F428), provided key copies are controlled.
**Why not the others**: B1-A leaves branch isolation to code review; B1-D contradicts part-1; B2-B adds revocation machinery and stale scope; B3-C leaves the key; B3-D mixes tenants; B4-C is not a binding.
**Follow-up record**: ADR needed — yes: (a) branch scoping and session model (inside the `cto` tenancy ADR), (b) local-store binding and re-registration/erase (inside the key-custody ADR). Threat model sections: branch isolation, re-registration. Spikes S41, S43 (and S42, S44, S45) PENDING.

#### Proposed pin changes

None. No version is adopted by this note; `pins.yaml` is untouched.

### Part 5 — Tenant export and deletion (PDPL, ETA retention); noisy-neighbour limits on sync

**Recommended - pending user decision** (the `cto` decides by ADR; counsel decides every legal reading; the founder decides the closure and device questions in § 9). ADR needed: **yes** — (1) retention/deletion/export, (2) sync limits and the limiter dependency.

**A. Export and deletion**
1. **Deletion: DEL-2**, with DEL-4's de-identification as a step and DEL-3 held back as a documented escalation. Shape: `active -> suspended -> closure_requested -> export_window -> purge_personal -> archive_retained(retain_until) -> purged`, each step logged with date and time (regulation Art. 4 Second 3 requires the date and time of erasure or delivery to the controller, F508). Owner-only, step-up authenticated, delayed by a cooling-off period so a stolen session cannot erase a venue (abuse case 1). Retention periods and the cooling-off length are data (`retain_until` per tenant, eta-note R3); no number is chosen here.
2. **Backups: let them expire inside the backup window, and replay deletions after any restore.** The backup window stays shorter than the 60-day device retention (OQ29); deleted-tenant and erased-person tombstones live in the control journal outside the restored database (part-3 D5-C) and are re-applied by the restore runbook, so a restore cannot bring back deleted personal data. Document the backup behaviour to the data subject through the controller (F522 comparison). Escalate to DEL-3 only if counsel says backups must be erased sooner.
3. **Roles (for counsel, F504, F508, F510):** treat the venue as controller and Kodelix as processor for staff data; Kodelix acts on the owner's written instruction (the product terms and the closure request), exports to the owner, erases and records the time. Kodelix also needs a record of processing, a DPO decision and licence/permit analysis before 2026-11-01 (F502, F510): this is an operational deadline, not a design choice.
4. **Export: EXP-2.** CSV per table with header, byte-exact signed payloads, manifest with SHA-256, data dictionary; excludes credentials; delivered to the authenticated owner only; the closure flow does not purge the tax archive until the owner has downloaded or the cooling-off ends `[A]`.
5. **Breach notice**: build the 72-hour clock into the incident runbook for Kodelix as processor (F509): detection time recorded, Centre portal notification, notice to the controller at once so the venue can notify staff within three business days.
6. **Suspended or deleted tenant and its devices** (design inference; no source, the founder decides): *suspended* = the server still accepts uploads of already-sold commands (never lose money facts) but refuses new enrolments and sends `tenant_suspended`; the device sells until its offline grace ends, then locks selling (N5, F525). *Closed* = the server answers `tenant_closed`; the device stops selling at the end of its grace and shows the owner the export instruction; it **does not wipe** local records automatically (the venue's own tax copy, 60 days); wipe is an explicit owner action after export. Both states use RFC 9457 problems with distinct `type` URIs (F516).

**B. Limits on the sync endpoint**
1. **Enforcement: RL-C layered**: coarse per-IP edge limit and body-size cap at the proxy before authentication (Traefik built-in is available, F520; avoid a custom Caddy build, F531); per-device and per-tenant limits in the application **after** the credential is verified, keyed only by identifiers taken from the credential (part-3 D3-A); in-process token bucket as the fast guard and fallback; shared counter in Valkey 9.1.2 or later, limiter account only, fail-open to the in-process limit on error.
2. **Algorithm: token bucket (ALG-2) or GCRA (ALG-3)**, chosen when the library question closes; not fixed window for the sync route. Count **commands and bytes as well as requests** (F528).
3. **Catch-up rule: limits delay, they never drop.** On excess, answer 429 with `Retry-After` and an RFC 9457 body; the device keeps its outbox and retries (outbox rows are deleted only on a parsed ack, F525); the server acks the prefix it accepted instead of rejecting a whole batch `[A]`; bucket capacity per plan is sized to the largest backlog the offline grace can produce, so the first sync after days offline passes within the burst and the rest drains at the steady rate; jitter on retries. Only non-money endpoints (export, reports, dashboard reads) have hard quotas.
4. **Headers:** `Retry-After` plus 429 (final standards, F515); emit `RateLimit`/`RateLimit-Policy` only as an optional extra and treat their syntax as unstable until the draft becomes an RFC (F514). No `X-RateLimit-*`.
5. **Limits are data:** `plan_limits(plan, key, value, unit)` and `tenant_limit_overrides(tenant, key, value, reason, expires_at)`; the effective value is override or plan default, read through a short-TTL cache with invalidation on change; keys (names are proposals): `sync.rate`, `sync.burst`, `sync.batch_max_commands`, `sync.batch_max_bytes`, `sync.concurrent_per_tenant`, `sync.concurrent_per_device`, `export.concurrent`, `db.statement_timeout_ms`. The registry `design/registry/entities.yaml` (plans, quotas) does not exist yet. No number is proposed: none is sourced.
6. **PostgreSQL:** timeouts are off by default (F518), so set `statement_timeout`, `lock_timeout`, `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` explicitly at role level as a ceiling and apply the per-plan value with `SET LOCAL` in the tenant transaction (the role is shared, F517); per-tenant connection share is an application bulkhead (semaphore per tenant in front of the pool), not `CONNECTION LIMIT` (per role); fairness of the inbox apply worker is round-robin across tenants `[A]` (not researched).

**Why the others lost:** DEL-1 destroys the tax copy; DEL-3 adds a key system before counsel asks for it; DEL-4 alone leaves backups and joins; EXP-1 leaks if one table lacks RLS and is not owner-readable; EXP-3 is slow and inconsistent for archives; RL-A cannot know the tenant; RL-B alone breaks with more than one instance; RL-D burns the database to protect it; fixed window punishes the legitimate post-outage burst.

**Conditions that reopen:** counsel reads the erasure right as reaching backups; Law 14/2025 or the tax rule forces identifiable retention (F513, F535); a measured limiter latency over budget (S51); tenancy model changes to schema/db-per-tenant (then role-per-tenant timeouts become possible, F517).

#### Proposed pin changes

None. `golang.org/x/time` v0.16.0 was flagged not-latest (F532) and the backend track is unset; Valkey stays at the pinned 9.1.2 (the patched version for F533).

### Part 6 — Cross-tenant negative test catalogue; ASVS / MASVS / API Top 10 mapping

**Chosen**: **Recommended - pending user decision**: A + E (+ D-instrumented for AN-02, AN-03, AN-05, AN-06 and D-static for AN-09/AN-10), with C deferred until a staging environment exists.

**Why**: API1's own prevention list asks for tests that block deploys (F610); the matrix makes "a new route without an isolation case" a build failure, and F621 shows a table or view added without the control leaks silently, which only the catalogue test sees. The wave-1 outcomes (credential-derived ids, composite keys, `FOR SHARE` ordering) are all observable from outside, so the SY tests stay valid whichever driver or model the cto picks (F623, pending).
**Why not the others**: B leaves gaps by construction; C has no sourced tool and cannot sign sync requests; D-static alone misses runtime caches and account-switch state; E alone misses application-layer defects (DA-03, DA-06).
**Follow-up record**: no ADR from this note. The test catalogue becomes acceptance criteria in stories (`/create-stories`) and input to `/qa-plan` and `/threat-model`; the tenancy ADR (cto) decides whether DB-xx change shape.

#### Proposed pin changes

None. No version is adopted. Testcontainers pins stay with part-2's proposal.

## 6. Security Notes

- **Controls**: ASVS 5.0.0 IDs were looked up in the official v5.0.0 files by parts 1, 3, 4, 5 and 6; part 6 holds the full mapping of the test catalogue to ASVS 5.0.0, MASVS 2.1.0 / MASTG and API Security Top 10 2023. Core: 8.4.1 (L2, cross-tenant), 8.2.2 and 8.3.1 (L1), 13.2.2 (L2, least-privilege DB accounts), 7.2.x and 7.4.1-7.4.2 (sessions), 6.4.x-6.6.x (enrolment code), 11.1.x-11.5.1 (key management), 14.1-14.3 (retention, data protection, client storage), 15.4.2 (L3, check-and-act atomicity), 16.2.5 / 16.3.3 / 16.4.1 / 16.5.3 (logging, fail securely); API1, API2, API3, API4, API5 (2023); MASVS-STORAGE-1/2, MASVS-AUTH-1, MASVS-PLATFORM-1/3, MASVS-CODE-4, MASVS-CRYPTO-2. Gaps in the standards: no ASVS item for "identity from the credential" (part-6 F602) and no MASVS/MASTG item for clearing the previous account's data on logout or switch (part-6 F609). Target ASVS level undecided (§ 4.2 item 9).
- **Advisories**: see § 4.2 item 12; each part records its search dates and the searches that did not run.
- **Abuse cases**: each part lists its own below; together they are the input to `/threat-model tenant-isolation` and to the proposed `docs/architecture/threat-models/local-first-sync.md`.
- **Threat model**: required (auth, crypto, PII, multi-tenant); neither threat model exists yet (`docs/architecture/` is absent).
- **Legal**: every PDPL and tax-law statement is a report of translated text and **needs counsel** (part 5). Time-bound: the PDPL executive regulation (Decree 816/2025, Gazette 244 Supplement A, 2025-11-01) gives, on a plain reading, a one-year compliance period ending **2026-11-01** (part-5 F501, F502; read from an unofficial English copy).

### Part 1 — PostgreSQL RLS re-verification at 18.6; tenancy model options

- **Controls** (ASVS 5.0.0, looked up in the official v5.0.0 text, F125): **8.4.1** (L2) cross-tenant controls — the requirement this decision exists to meet; **8.2.2** (L1) data-specific access (object-level); **8.3.1** (L1) enforcement at a trusted layer (RLS plus the application layer, not the client); **8.1.1** (L1) documented authorization rules; **8.4.2** (L3) layered administrative access — relevant to the platform-operator path that can see more than one tenant; **13.2.2** (L2) least-privilege database accounts (runtime roles without ownership, BYPASSRLS or superuser, F103); **13.3.1/13.3.2** (L2) database credentials in a secret store with least-privilege access; **13.2.3** (L2) no default credentials (relevant to the image's bootstrap superuser). Logging and 15.4.2 IDs: NOT SOURCEABLE in this part (F125).
- **Advisories** (searched 2026-10-17): PostgreSQL 18.6 — the vendor security page lists none affecting or fixed after 18.6; CVE-2026-14666 (RLS caching after role changes) and CVE-2026-19385 (`pg_dump` heap overflow, so the export tool must be 18.6 or later) are fixed in 18.6 (F124). PgBouncer — 1.26.1 (2026-10-14) fixes CVE-2026-107350 (SCRAM impersonation of other users including admins) and two others; the tested 1.26.0 predates them (F114); the repository shows no published GHSA. pgx 5.11.0 — not re-searched here (drivers are another part); restaurant-menu B27 reports OSV clean on 2026-10-14. OSV for PostgreSQL and PgBouncer: could not run (F129f).
- **Abuse cases** (inputs for the threat model): (1) a developer adds a tenant table without FORCE or a policy — blocked by catalogue queries C1/C2 in CI (F111); (2) a tenant plants a reference to another tenant's row through a single-column FK and probes existence by error difference (F106); (3) a tenant probes another tenant's values through a non-tenant-leading unique key (F105); (4) a handler uses session-level `SET`/`set_config(...,false)` and the next client on the same pooled server connection inherits that tenant (F115, executed leak); (5) a handler sets the tenant twice in one transaction (F110); (6) a view over tenant tables owned by a bypass-capable role (F108); (7) an export or backup job running as a bypass role, or an RLS-model export that includes a table without a policy, exposes every tenant (F117); (8) restoring the cluster to recover one tenant resurrects other tenants' deleted data and conflicts with device sequence numbers (F118, F127); (9) migration fan-out leaves tenants on different schema versions while the application assumes one (B, C; F120); (10) if PgBouncer is exposed, the pre-1.26.1 SCRAM flaw lets an attacker impersonate other database users (F114); (11) the platform operator or support staff reading across tenants with a privileged role — needs a named, audited path (8.4.2).
- **Threat model**: required (auth, PII, tenant isolation): `docs/architecture/threat-models/tenant-isolation.md` via `/threat-model`; not yet written in this repo. The sibling threat model was not read.
- **Legal**: PDPL erasure versus backups versus 5-year ETA retention — **needs counsel**; no legal text was fetched or interpreted (F126).

### Part 2 — Tenant context per backend track (pgx; pgjdbc, HikariCP, Exposed, jOOQ); edge proxy (Caddy, Traefik)

- **Controls**: ASVS IDs were not looked up in this part (F291). Reused, not re-fetched: restaurant-menu C21 (4.1.3), C22 (16.2.5), C23 (13.2.2), B6 (8.1.1, 8.2.2, 8.4.1). `security-lead` to confirm text before the threat model cites them.
- **Advisories**: search did not run for this part's versions (F290). Previously searched 2026-10-14 by restaurant-menu: pgx 5.11.0 none; Caddy per C20a–d. pgjdbc, HikariCP, Exposed, jOOQ, Traefik: not searched. Owner `appsec-engineer`.
- **Abuse cases** (each with the evidence that bounds it):
  1. A forged `X-Tenant-*` or forwarding header reaches the backend: stripped by Caddy `-X-Tenant*` (F263); Traefik only for listed names (F269); backend must ignore (X3).
  2. Client spoofs its IP through a trusted-range misconfiguration (F266).
  3. A use case forgets the context or uses a pool connection outside the helper: 0 rows / 42501 (F207, K1).
  4. A developer uses session-level `set_config` or a bare `ThreadLocal`: leaks across requests (F210, F235c, F240); mitigated by code review rule, the `PrepareConn`/`DataSource` guard alarm and the leak tests.
  5. Nested Exposed transaction block switches the outer tenant (F239/K5).
  6. A tenant-controlled value ends up in `set_config`: it is a bound parameter; malformed value raises 22P02 (F212); parse to UUID in application code.
  7. Spoofed values and slugs recorded in proxy logs (F271, F272).
- **Threat model**: required (auth, PII, tenant isolation): `docs/architecture/threat-models/tenant-isolation.md`, to be written by `security-lead`; not created here.

Test that proves "forgot the context fails closed", per track (names exist in the evidence, ready to be ported):
- Go: `TestGo_ForgottenContextFailsClosed` (0 rows on SELECT, 42501 on INSERT as a non-owner NOBYPASSRLS role) plus `TestGo_LocalContextDoesNotSurviveCommitOrRollback` and `TestGo_SessionLevelSetConfigLeaks_NegativeControl` (proves the harness can see a leak). Pool size 1 so the next borrower is the same backend.
- JVM: spike checks K1a/K1b (forgotten), K2b/K3b/K7c (next borrower clean), K4a/K4b (ThreadLocal leak demonstration), K5 (nested), K6c (negative control). Run through PgBouncer for the pooler variants (S21 tests `TestPooler_*`, S24 K8).
Both must connect as the runtime role; a superuser connection passes vacuously (`postgres/best-practices.md` testing paragraph).

### Part 3 — Device enrolment and credential; sync request scoping; server-side sync state

- **Controls (ASVS 5.0.0, looked up in the official v5.0.0 files, 2026-10-17; F307-F309):**
  - Authorization and tenancy: 8.1.1 (L1), 8.2.1 (L1), 8.2.2 (L1, IDOR/BOLA), 8.3.1 (L1, enforcement in a trusted layer), 8.3.2 (L3, immediate effect of authorization changes, relevant to revocation), **8.4.1 (L2, the only multi-tenant requirement)**, 8.1.3 / 8.1.4 (L3, document device as a contextual attribute).
  - Enrolment code: 6.4.1 (L1), 6.5.1, 6.5.3, 6.5.4 (at least 20 bits), 6.5.5 (at most 10 minutes), 6.6.2, 6.6.3 (rate limit) (all L2).
  - Device key and revocation: 6.5.6 (L3, revoke a factor after loss), 6.7.1 and 6.7.2 (L3), 6.4.4 (L2, replacement needs proofing at enrolment level), 6.3.7 (L3, notify on credential change).
  - Transport: 12.1.3 (L2) and 12.1.4 / 12.3.5 (L3) if mTLS is used.
  - Logging (looked up in the sync note, F436 there; not re-read): 16.2.5, 16.3.3, 16.4.1, 16.4.2.
  - **Not found:** an ASVS requirement stating "identity comes from the credential, not the request" (F310). API Security Top 10 2023: API1 and API2 IDs are in sync-note F324 (not re-read); MASVS 2.1.0 not applicable (no mobile client in this part; the owner Android app is outside this part's two areas).
- **Advisories (searched 2026-10-17):** PostgreSQL security page: nothing listed as fixed after 18.6; CVE-2026-14666 (row-security caching vs role changes), CVE-2026-16238 / 16239 (type confusion, CVSS 8.8), CVE-2026-6470 are fixed in 18.6 (F315). The GHSA/OSV record for PostgreSQL itself was not searched (not a package-manager artefact); the security page is the vendor record. `yaronf/httpsign`: GHSA-6wwm-chj6-h3p2 (high, 2026-07-14, crafted `Signature-Input` panics the verifier; affected range not read) (F332). pgx 5.11.0, ktor-server-core 3.6.0, exposed-core 1.5.1: OSV clean on 2026-10-16 in the sync note (F315 row) and for pgx on 2026-10-14 in restaurant-menu B27; **not re-run today**. No RFC 9421 or DPoP library version is chosen, so no OSV query for one was possible.
- **Abuse cases (feed `/threat-model`):**
  1. A payload names another tenant, branch or device (forged ids): refused, event under the credential's tenant (F322).
  2. An attacker with a valid key floods mismatch events to fill the table and alert the owner: aggregate and rate-limit events and alerts per device; store claimed ids as bounded, escaped text (F322 note).
  3. An attacker sends bad signatures with a **known `key_id`** of tenant A to make the owner alert fire: the tenant is derived from an unauthenticated field in that case, so such refusals go to the platform sink and a per-device counter, not to a per-request tenant event (P3-OQ2).
  4. A leaked or intercepted enrolment code is redeemed first: the approval step with a fingerprint comparison (D1-A) is the control; brute force of the code at the unauthenticated redeem endpoint: entropy, TTL, attempt cap, rate limit (F306, F309).
  5. A captured signed request is replayed: the inbox dedupes commands (F323), but non-idempotent endpoints (enrolment, key staging, reads of the feed) need the nonce or `created` rule (F302).
  6. A device clock that is wrong or rolled back defeats `created`-based freshness: use a server nonce (F302), tied to sync-note Part D (P3-OQ8).
  7. A disk or VM clone carries the same key: only a non-exportable TPM key or a rolling server-issued token (sync-note S109) detects it; seq conflicts catch it after the fact (F323). DPAPI user or machine scope does not stop a local administrator (F318).
  8. A revoked or stolen device keeps syncing: `FOR SHARE` ordering and a `revoked_credential_used` event (F329); in-flight commands may still commit before the revoke if the path does not take the lock (F329 case 1).
  9. A restore resurrects a revoked device or loses a newly enrolled one (F330): the control journal and the runbook replay (D5-C).
  10. A cross-tenant actor (the backup-verification job) writes the wrong watermark and makes devices prune records the server cannot recover: monotonic trigger and explicit single-purpose policy (F328), plus authority from the backup catalogue (P3-OQ6).
  11. Uniqueness-error probing across tenants: tenant-leading keys (sync-note F128) and the `(tenant_id, ...)` composite keys here (F326).
  12. A crafted header crashes the signature verifier (F332): wrap the verifier, fuzz it, cap header and body size.
- **Threat model:** required — `docs/architecture/threat-models/local-first-sync.md` (proposed in the sync note, not yet written; `docs/architecture/` does not exist in this repo). The enrolment/credential/revocation flow and the restore replay should each be a section of it.
- **Legal (needs counsel):** no legal claim is made. Device ids, key ids and public-key fingerprints are not shown to be personal data; whether they count as personal data of staff under PDPL (Law 151/2020) was not researched (NOT SOURCEABLE — primary text not fetched).

### Part 4 — Branch scoping and session; encrypted local store binding and re-registration

- **Controls** (ASVS 5.0.0, looked up in the official v5.0.0 files, 2026-10-17): authorization/tenancy 8.1.1, 8.2.1, 8.2.2 (L1, branch = data-specific access), 8.3.1 (L1), 8.4.1 (L2) (part-1 F125, part-3 F307); sessions **7.2.1, 7.2.2, 7.2.3, 7.2.4, 7.4.1, 7.4.2** (L1), 7.1.2, 7.4.5 (L2) (F410); self-contained tokens if B2-B/C is chosen **9.1.1-9.1.3** (L1), **9.2.1** (L1), **9.2.2-9.2.4** (L2) (F411); data protection **14.1.1, 14.1.2, 14.2.4** (L2), **14.2.7** (L3), **14.3.1** (L1, SPA), 14.3.3 (L2, SPA) (F415); cryptography **11.1.1, 11.1.2, 11.2.2, 11.3.3** (L2), 11.5.1 (L2) (F416). API Security Top 10 2023: **API5** (F412); API1 per sync-note F324. **MASVS 2.1.0: NOT SOURCEABLE** (F432). Not found: an ASVS requirement ending sessions on role change (F410), one on key destruction (F416), one on "scope never from the client" (F411, part-3 F310).
- **Advisories** (searched 2026-10-17): PostgreSQL 18.6 — none unfixed per the vendor page (part-1 F124; not re-fetched). SQLCipher — GHSA shows none published (F431); 4.19.0 fixes two unnamed low-risk issues (desktop-note F102). SQLite3MC, Willena sqlite-jdbc, Tauri/Electron/Compose Desktop: not searched (versions not pinned). OSV: could not run (POST-only for the fetch tool).
- **Abuse cases**: (1) a manager of A1 sends requests naming branch A2 in path, query, body or a stale token: refused or filtered by the server-derived set and RLS (F418); (2) a code path sets the scope twice or sets `branch_all` for a non-owner: widened scope (F420); (3) a manager is reassigned or demoted but a long-lived token still carries the old scope (B2-B); (4) a materialized view, reporting job or export runs as a bypass role and serves another branch or tenant (F423, part-1 F108/F117); (5) a manager inserts himself into `staff_branches` (F421: policy plus app check); (6) a per-branch unique key (order numbers) or single-column FK leaks sibling-branch existence (F418 note, S45 pending); (7) a PC is handed to a new tenant without a drain: the old tenant's unsynced sales are destroyed silently, or the new tenant opens the old store (B3-A gate, B4-A refusal); (8) the re-registration wipe is used by an insider as the OQ27 erase-offline-sales route (sync-note OQ27): the drain gate and the owner "device reset" event are the controls; (9) the old store is recovered from a shadow copy, a backup or free pages after erase and the wrapped key survives in a copy (F425, F426, F428, F404); (10) a copied store opened on another PC or by another Windows user (TS-04); (11) a stale store bound to tenant A is opened by a build holding tenant B's credential: refuse (B4-A) and never recreate (TS-05); (12) the export file of B3-B is carried out of the venue or imported into the wrong tenant.
- **Threat model**: required — `docs/architecture/threat-models/tenant-isolation.md` (`/threat-model`; `docs/architecture/` does not exist yet) with sections for branch isolation and device re-registration; also feeds the sync threat model.
- **Legal (needs counsel)**: staff attendance and passcodes are personal data under the PDPL in Tier 1 (F407); whether a branch manager seeing another branch's staff names, or erasure of a device store holding personal data, has legal requirements: needs counsel; no legal text fetched.

### Part 5 — Tenant export and deletion (PDPL, ETA retention); noisy-neighbour limits on sync

- **Controls** (ASVS 5.0.0 official text, F527): 14.1.1, 14.1.2, 14.2.4, 14.2.7 (retention and automatic deletion), 14.2.6 (minimum data in the export), 2.4.1 (anti-automation), 2.1.3 and 2.3.2 (documented and implemented business-logic limits); tenant-isolation IDs 8.4.1, 8.2.2, 13.2.2 from part-1 F125. API Security Top 10 2023 **API4:2023** (F528). Backup coverage of retention is not an ASVS 14 line (F527). V11 key-management and V16 logging IDs: NOT SOURCEABLE.
- **Advisories** (searched 2026-10-17): Valkey — GHSA-fq2f-crmw-q97r (Lua UAF, <= 9.1.1, patched 9.1.2); the page-1 list has nine others (RDMA, TLS, stream, RESP) not opened, page 2 not read (F533). PostgreSQL 18.6: see part-1 F124. `golang.org/x/time`: OSV query returned no records (F534, tier E). Redis 8.2, Traefik 3.7.14, pgBackRest: not searched. GHSA for x/time and OSV for Valkey: not run.
- **Abuse cases:** (1) a stolen owner session requests closure to destroy a venue's data: step-up auth, cooling-off, notification to every owner contact; (2) an attacker with an owner session pulls the full export (staff PII, sales): step-up, short-lived link, download audit, rate cap on exports; (3) the export misses a table or includes another tenant's rows: coverage test and RLS-role execution (F530, part-1 F117); (4) a restore resurrects a deleted tenant or an erased person: tombstone replay (A.2); (5) the closure purge deletes rows the tax duty needs: archive is separate and purge refuses while `retain_until` is in the future or a legal hold is set; (6) a device of a closed tenant keeps selling forever or is wiped before export: bounded grace and no auto-wipe (A.6); (7) a noisy tenant exhausts the pool or the inbox worker: per-tenant bulkhead, fair worker, `statement_timeout`; (8) batch stuffing defeats a per-request limit (F528): count commands and bytes; (9) a limiter key from an unverified header drains a victim's bucket: keys only from the verified credential; (10) retry storm after an outage: `Retry-After` with jitter; (11) the limiter store goes down and blocks all sales sync: fail-open; (12) any client able to run `EVAL` on Valkey reaches the Lua UAF class (F533): ACL; (13) a 429 or a timeout is read as a failed command and the outbox row is dropped: sync-note rule, delete on parsed ack only; (14) breach not reported in 72 hours (F509).
- **Threat model:** required (PII, auth, tenant isolation): `docs/architecture/threat-models/tenant-isolation.md` via `/threat-model`; not written. These abuse cases are inputs.
- **Legal:** all statements are reports of translated text (F501-F513, F535, F536); **needs counsel**.
